# Godfried Schalcken: Study Notes

**Site:** Godfried Schalcken (8 October 1643, Made - 16 November 1706, The Hague; the Leiden Collection gives 13 November 1706), Dutch painter; the candlelight specialist, the first fijnschilder from outside Leiden, whose kaarslichtjes -- small nocturnal interiors lit by a single candle -- were, in the Leiden Collection's judgement, unsurpassed in their rendering of artificial light. He was trained under Samuel van Hoogstraten in Dordrecht from 1656, aged thirteen, then under Gerrit Dou in Leiden c. 1663; back in Dordrecht until 1691, in London 1692-1697, in The Hague from 1698, and on a brief, honoured sojourn in Dusseldorf in 1703, where Johann Wilhelm, Elector Palatine, gave him the gold chain he wears in his last self-portrait. His pupil Arnold Houbraken wrote his life; his sister Maria was his first pupil; his nephew Jacob painted so like him that the works are still mistaken for his. He left no writing of his own: every word quoted about him in this study is someone else's.

**Doorway:** Hop from the Aert de Gelder study: the father wrote the book, and the pupil he wrote about stayed in Dordrecht and kept Rembrandt's manner alive there for sixty years -- now meet the man who learned from the same Dordrecht master, Samuel van Hoogstraten, and then made a whole oeuvre out of a single light source. De Gelder kept the master's manner; Schalcken kept the master's teacher's other pupil's light -- Dou's candle -- and turned it into a career, a reputation, and a court. The doorway is the single light source.

One-hundred-and-sixty-ninth doorway check in a row. ## The life

Godfried Schalcken was born in Made, near Geertruidenberg in North Brabant, in 1643 -- 8 October by Wikipedia alone; every other source read gives the year only (F1, C3). The older tradition says Dordrecht: Houbraken himself writes that he "was born in the year 1643 in Dordrecht", and Bryan's Dictionary agrees -- but the modern scholarship (Leiden Collection, Wikipedia, National Galleries of Scotland) says Made, and Houbraken most likely elided birthplace with the town where the boy grew up (C1). He was the son of Cornelis Schalcken (1610-74), a clergyman, and Aletta Lydius (1612-78), "who came from a respected family of preachers and theologians" (F2). Cornelis was parish minister in Eethen and Drongelen, then in Drimmelen and Made from 1643 to 1654; the family moved to Dordrecht in 1654, when Cornelis became rector of the Latin school there (F3). Wikipedia instead says the move happened "before he was four years old" -- unsourced, and inconsistent with the father's posting history; the 1654 date is the sourced one (C15). Cornelis and Aletta married in Heusden in 1636 and had eight children (F4).

In 1656, aged thirteen, Schalcken was apprenticed to his fellow townsman Samuel van Hoogstraten (1627-78) in Dordrecht (F7). Around 1663 he went to study under Gerrit Dou (1613-75) in Leiden (F8). The Dou dates are approximate -- Leiden Collection "around 1663", Getty ULAN return to Dordrecht 1665, Sotheby's "almost certainly trained with Gerard Dou until 1665", Wikipedia's "worked in Leiden until c. 1675" contradicting all the others and not used here (C4). Exactly when he returned to Dordrecht is unknown; "it was presumably shortly after he completed his training. In any case, he was living in Dordrecht again around 1669" (F11). His early genre pictures "very closely resemble Dou's work" (F12) -- but the Leiden Collection stresses the limit: Dou's influence stayed in the technique, and the style Schalcken actually assimilated was Frans van Mieris the Elder's.

His younger sister Maria Schalcken (c. 1645 - before 1700) became his first pupil: she "presumably studied with her brother from 1665 to 1670" after he returned from Leiden, gave up painting on her marriage in 1682, and only two paintings can be firmly attributed to her -- her Self-Portrait at the Easel and Boy Offering Grapes to a Woman (F5). Her self-portrait was attributed to her brother until a twentieth-century cleaning revealed her full signature. Historians suspect some works currently attributed to Godfried may be hers. His nephew Jacobus (Jakob) Schalcken (born c. 1681-82) was also his pupil and painted in the same style, "so that his works have been mistaken for those of his uncle" (F6) -- cousin or nephew is unresolved across sources (F5 in leg 3: EN Wikipedia says cousin, Bryan's and Hofstede de Groot say nephew).

In October 1679 Schalcken married Francoisia (Francoise) van Diemen (1661-1744) of Breda (F13). The couple had seven children, of whom only their daughter Francoisia (b. 1690) reached adulthood (F14). There was no son. His 1679 self-portrait and the portrait of Francoise van Diemen of the same year form a marriage pendant pair (F15).

After Nicolaes Maes moved to Amsterdam in 1673, Schalcken became Dordrecht's leading portrait painter (F16). In 1676 he painted portraits of the wealthy Leiden cloth merchant Pieter de la Court (1618-1685) and four members of his family. From the 1680s he travelled regularly to The Hague for portrait commissions from the circles around first Stadholder and then, from 1689, King William III. He joined the Hague painters' society Pictura in 1691, although he continued to reside in Dordrecht (F17).

Around 1692 he crossed to London with his family and established himself there (F18). Houbraken says he met with great success and the English elite was eager to sit for him; Jacob Campo Weyerman, who disliked his work, said the English soon tired of portraits "as flat as unrisen pancakes" and that he was forced "to paint history scenes and 'night lights'". "Whether Schalcken actually had such a dearth of patrons in London is unknown, but it is true that very few portraits by Schalcken can be linked to his London years" (Leiden Collection). Wikipedia's blunter version: in England "his uncouth manners and bad temper alienated him from the society there" (F19). He was back in the Dutch Republic in June 1698 and settled in The Hague (F20).

In his final years his most important patrons were Elector Palatine Johann Wilhelm II (1658-1716), Danish king Frederick IV (1671-1730) and Prussian king Frederick I (1657-1713) (F21). The contact with the Elector "led to a brief sojourn in Dusseldorf in 1703. During this visit Johann Wilhelm presented Schalcken with a gold chain in gratitude for his service, which he depicted in his self-portrait of 1706" (Leiden Collection). Wikipedia's "employed by Johann Wilhelm" overstates it: a brief, honoured visit, not a formal employment (C17). He died in The Hague in 1706 -- 16 November per Wikipedia and Houbraken, 13 November per the Leiden Collection (F22, C2). His 1706 self-portrait, wearing the Elector's gold chain, "was to be his last self-portrait".

No connection to Gerard de Lairesse was found in any source read (F23, C593). De Lairesse (1640-1711) was a Liege-born classicist painter active in Amsterdam; he does not appear in Schalcken's training line anywhere. The documented path is van Hoogstraten (Dordrecht, from 1656) to Dou (Leiden, c. 1663) to Dordrecht to The Hague to London 1692-1697 to Dusseldorf 1703.

Together with Eglon van der Neer (1635/36-1703) and Caspar Netscher (1639-84), Schalcken was "among the most important representatives of a third generation of fijnschilders (fine painters) after Dou and Frans van Mieris (1635-81). Schalcken was the first fijnschilder from outside of Leiden" (F24). His reputation "at home and far beyond the boundaries of the Dutch Republic rested on his kaarslichtjes, or pictures with figures in a nocturnal setting illuminated by artificial light. The artist's rendering of artificial light was unsurpassed" (F25). Houbraken's verdict: "He was one of the happiest of Dutch painters, seeing that the art of his brush was amply paid from the beginning to the end of his life, so that he reaped the rewards of his labours while alive, which occurs but very rarely" (F26, Q5).

Afterlife: his atmospheric work inspired Sheridan Le Fanu's gothic horror story "Strange Event in the Life of Schalken the Painter", adapted by the BBC as Schalcken the Painter (broadcast 23 December 1979, Omnibus) (F27). The first book in English devoted to his whole output is Wayne Franits, Godefridus Schalcken: A Late 17th-century Dutch Painter in Pursuit of Fame and Fortune (2016/2018); Thierry Beherman's catalogue raisonne is Godfried Schalcken (Paris, 1988) (F28).

## M1. The single candle as the only sun

What it IS: one small, warm, near light source inside a dark room; everything else is shadow. Value is organised radially around the flame: the flame is the brightest point, faces and hands nearest it are the second-brightest, and the rest of the room falls off steeply into near-black. How it shows: in the William III the king's face and armour catch the flame while the background is void; in Young Girl with a Candle the girl's face is the picture; in the Uffizi self-portrait the artist's face and hand emerge from black.

**Takeaway:** one light decides what exists. A piece built on this mechanism must have exactly one source, and everything in the frame must be placed by its distance from that source -- nothing lit for its own sake.

## M2. The fixed-viewpoint dark-room study (peephole tradition)

What it IS: per Bryan's, the model is set in a dark room with a candle, observed through a small hole, and painted by daylight from that fixed observation. How it shows: the lighting in Schalcken's candle pieces is unnaturally consistent -- one viewpoint, one frozen flame position, no wandering highlights -- which is why they read as "natural" rather than theatrical. Status: anecdote ("he is said to have"), not documented fact.

**Takeaway:** the light must be fixed before it can be painted. A flickering flame cannot be transcribed from life; it must be frozen -- by a peephole, a dark room, or sheer slow looking -- and then rebuilt in thin, patient layers. For a generative piece, the lesson is: lock the light first, then let everything else move around it.

## M3. Diffuse soft modelling (the flattering light)

What it IS: candlelight close to the face models softly because the flame is an extended source; edges between light and shadow are graded, not hard. How it shows: the Leiden Collection's phrase -- the soft modelling gives the sitter "an amiable and elegant impression" -- is the commercial point: candlelight portraits flatter, so the rich paid for them. Compare Weyerman's hostile counter-reading, that the London portraits were "as flat as unrisen pancakes" -- the same softness, judged as a fault.

**Takeaway:** softness is a product. The same graded falloff reads as elegance to one audience and flatness to another -- the piece should know which side of that judgement it is on, and choose its audience.

## M4. The interrogation shine (candle in the eyes)

What it IS: a figure holds a candle up to another figure's face so the light strikes the eyes directly, forcing the face to react -- boldness in the holder, hesitation in the lit face. How it shows: Houbraken's description of the lost Denial of St Peter: "The boldness of the maid servant, who shines a candle into his eyes, and the hesitancy and shyness of Peter, could clearly be discerned in the facial features." A light that is also a dramatic action.

**Takeaway:** light as an act, not a setting. The candle is pointed at someone -- the piece should let the viewer feel the light being aimed, and the face flinching from it.

## M5. Flesh looming through light / through cloth

What it IS: figures backlit by candlelight, daylight, or sun through a cloth, so flesh is revealed by transmitted/reflected light rather than by direct modelling. How it shows: Houbraken (p. 177): figures "loom up through candle- or daylight, or lit by the sun through a cloth, so that the flesh might reveal itself more pleasantly through the clear reflection."

**Takeaway:** the body arrives through the light, not before it. A figure should emerge out of the glow -- half-formed at the edge of it -- rather than standing fully lit in front of it.

## M6. Two-light collision

What it IS: two different light sources in one picture -- moon + candle, or sunlight + charcoal fire -- each with its own colour temperature and falloff. How it shows: Hofstede de Groot (p. 326) records these as Schalcken's admired experiments; they are the one place he breaks the single-source rule, and the clash of cool moonlight against warm candlelight is the whole picture.

**Takeaway:** one light is the rule; two lights is the event. A piece on this mechanism should hold the single source for most of its run and let the second light arrive as a collision -- cool against warm -- then resolve or fail.

## M7. The enamel surface (fijnschilder build)

What it IS: many thin, finely ground, translucent layers over a muted dead colour on a chalk-grounded panel, no wet blending, no visible brushstroke; the finish reads as enamel. How it shows: the "neatness of his finishing" and "mellow, but too fused" touch (Bryan's); the month spent on a single tapestry in Lady, Come into the Garden (Houbraken). Caveat: documented technically for Dou; Schalcken inherited it and later loosened it ("abandoned fine painting", NGS).

**Takeaway:** the surface is the labour made visible -- or rather, the labour made invisible. A piece on this mechanism should hide its own work: no brushstroke, no noise, only the slow accumulation of thin light.

## M8. The self-portrait as advertisement (vidimus)

What it IS: a highly finished drawing/painting sent to a patron for approval before the commission, doubling as self-promotion. How it shows: Schalcken suggested his own inclusion in Cosimo III's Uffizi self-portrait gallery, proposed the unprecedented nighttime setting ("the gallery of self-portraits as yet contained no others set at night"), and sent a finished drawing as a vidimus plus a mezzotint of his Mary Magdalene to please the print-collecting Grand Duke (Peck Collection). He holds a print, not a painting, in the portrait -- a deliberate pitch to a collector's taste.

**Takeaway:** the portrait is a pitch. The sitter is selling himself to a specific buyer, and every prop is chosen for that buyer's taste -- a piece on this mechanism should show the selling, not just the face.

## M9. The portrait-within-the-picture (friends as models)

What it IS: genre scenes populated by recognisable portraits -- the artist himself, his sister, his circle -- so a "game" picture is also a group portrait. How it shows: in Lady, Come into the Garden Schalcken painted himself "sitting undressed down to his shirt and underpants in the lap of a damsel. The other figures are also portraits and were known to everyone at the time" (Houbraken Translated, p. 176).

**Takeaway:** the game is a pretext for the people. The scene's figures are not types but named faces the room would recognise -- a piece on this mechanism should make the viewer feel they are intruding on a real party, not watching actors.

## M10. The curtain / tapestry as frame and labour-showpiece

What it IS: a heavy textile (curtain, tapestry, carpet-hanging) used to frame the composition and to display virtuoso rendering of fabric. How it shows: Sotheby's notes Schalcken "repeated some of the artistic formulas developed by [Dou] such as the use of a curtain to frame the composition"; Houbraken records a month's work on the tapestry in Lady, Come into the Garden.

**Takeaway:** the frame is the flex. A curtain pulled aside does two jobs at once -- it opens the scene for the viewer and it proves the painter can paint cloth. The piece should spend its craft budget on the thing that frames the light.

## M11. The tease: a flame about to die

What it IS: a small narrative built on a candle's fragility -- a boy blowing at a girl's candle, a girl blowing out a taper -- where the picture's light source is under threat inside the story. How it shows: A boy tries to blow out a girl's candle (1699-1706, Alte Pinakothek); Hofstede no. 259 Teasing: "A young girl with a lighted candle in her right hand. A young man standing behind her tries to blow the candle out. An effective picture." The light that makes the picture is also the thing the picture is about to lose.

**Takeaway:** threaten the light and the viewer leans in. The whole picture depends on a flame one breath could kill -- a piece on this mechanism should make the flame's survival the stakes, and let the viewer hold their own breath.

## M12. The mirror by candlelight (doubled light)

What it IS: a woman at her toilet with a candle and a mirror -- the flame lights the face, the mirror returns a second, dimmer image of it. How it shows: A Young Lady at Her Toilet (Mauritshuis 159): candle on the toilet-table, the lady attaching an ear-ring while looking into a mirror held by an old woman, a gentleman adding an ostrich feather. Two reflections of one flame, three people arranged around it.

**Takeaway:** one flame, two faces. The mirror doubles the light without adding a source -- a piece on this mechanism should let the viewer watch the same light arrive twice, once direct and once returned, dimmer and later.

## Works

### W1. The Annunciation -- 1660s -- J. Paul Getty Museum, Los Angeles (inv. 86.PB.464)

Early history/religious subject from the 1660s, i.e. the Leiden/Dordrecht years. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED (no Getty object page was read end to end; only the Wikidata list entry was seen).

### W2. An Old Woman Scouring a Pot -- 1660s -- National Gallery, London (NG997)

Small daylight domestic-labour scene; the National Gallery's own room text calls it a calm, virtuous-labour image whose objects on the sill give the scouring "a different, darker significance". One of his earliest documented genre types (his first recorded painting, 1673 inventory, was a kitchen with a maid cleaning fish). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED (National Gallery object page not reached; Wikidata list gives unclear figures "117 20", not usable).

### W3. Woman Holding an Egg up against a Lamp -- 1660s -- Gemaldegalerie Alte Meister, Dresden (Gal.-Nr. 1790)

Early candlelight test-piece: a woman holds an egg against a lamp so the light shines through it -- light as an investigative tool, not just atmosphere. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED.

### W4. Old Man Writing -- 1668 -- Louvre, Paris (INV 1832, MR 1017)

A scholar-at-work subject in the Dou lineage. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED (Wikidata list shows unclear figures "100 94", not usable).

### W5. A Girl with a Candle Drawing aside a Curtain -- 1670 -- Royal Collection, UK (RCIN 404624)

A girl pulls a curtain aside with a candle in hand -- the curtain device recurs across his work (threshold, revelation, the viewer caught looking). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED.

### W6. The Game of 'Lady, Come into the Garden' (Vrouwtje kom ten Hoof) -- c. 1670s, Dordrecht -- Royal Collection, UK (RCIN 405343; Buckingham Palace)

Houbraken singled this out as representative of his whole oeuvre. Schalcken portrayed himself stripped to shirt and underwear seated against a girl's lap; the other faces are portraits recognised at the time (the girl behind is said to be his sister Maria); Houbraken says he worked a month on the tapestry in the painting. A Dordrecht parlour game: the young man fails tests and surrenders items of clothing. Date conflict flagged: Wikidata lists 1670; the Houbraken/RCT account places its renown in Dordrecht in the 1670s. Dated here c. 1670s. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED (RCT object page returned footer chrome only).

### W7. The Penitent Magdalen by Candlelight -- 1670 -- Royal Collection, UK (RCIN 402596; Hampton Court)

Hofstede de Groot (vol. 5, no. 47) describes a half-length Magdalen by candlelight, seated at a table looking up at a crucifix. Schalcken painted the Magdalen many times (see Beyond the 20); this entry is the Royal Collection version only. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED (Hofstede de Groot gives no dimensions for no. 47 in the passage seen).

### W8. 'Every One His Fancy' (Elk zijn meug) -- 1670-1675 -- Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (SK-A-368)

A boy eats an egg messily and points at another boy spooning porridge; a bearded old man with spectacles and a child look on; a paper top right carries the English motto "Every one his fancy". Pendant of SK-A-369. A daylight moralising scene -- appetite, folly, watching -- with no candle at all, which matters for the evolution story. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED on the museum page. Reported-but-unverified: none found for this work (Hofstede de Groot no. 242, the same picture, gives panel 17 x 12 inches -- seen only in a search snippet, not read end to end, so it stays reported, not verified).

### W9. Girl Threading a Needle by Candlelight -- 1670s -- Wallace Collection, London (P171)

A young woman in profile, leaning on a table, threading a needle in front of a burning wick, work-box beside her. The subject/composition derives from a painting by Dou himself. Hofstede de Groot no. 195: panel, 7.5 x 6 inches. Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- 7.5 x 6 in. from Hofstede de Groot (1913 catalogue raisonne, seen in search snippet only; Wallace object page not read end to end).

### W10. Young Girl with a Candle -- 1670s -- Galleria Palatina (Palazzo Pitti), Florence (Uffizi inv. 1118)

The single-girl-with-candle type that became his signature: girl turns to the viewer, candle in her right hand. Note: at least three other versions of the Girl with a Candle composition exist on different supports (panel, copper, canvas), so "the" Uffizi version must not be conflated with the Leiden Collection follower copy (GS-101, dendro-dated after 1727, not by Schalcken). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED.

### W11. A Young Lady Weighing Cherries in a Shop -- c. 1680, Dordrecht -- National Gallery of Victoria, Melbourne (acc. 2026.15)

Oil on wood panel. A boy buys sweet cherries in an upmarket shop; the saleswoman wears blue silk trimmed with ermine. Formerly in the Demidoff collection, San Donato Palace, Florence. Signed in brown paint lower right "G (reversed) S". Dimensions: VERIFIED -- 36.0 x 27.4 cm (NGV object page).

### W12. Lady at a Mirror by Candlelight -- also called Toilet by Candle / Lady at her Toilet -- 17th century (undated) -- Mauritshuis, The Hague (inv. 159)

A lady at her toilet/mirror by candlelight. The Mauritshuis object page failed to render (returned the collection index, 47 lines -- no artwork data). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED (Wikidata list shows unclear figures "105 235 68", not usable).

### W13. A Girl with a Burning Candle -- 1687 -- Gemaldegalerie Alte Meister, Dresden (Gal.-Nr. 1787)

One of seven Schalckens in Dresden; the late-1680s date puts it at the height of his candlelight period, just before the English move. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED.

### W14. The Doctor's Examination (Het onderzoek van de dokter) -- c. 1690 -- Mauritshuis, The Hague

A sobbing young woman while the quack ("piskijker", piss-looker) examines her urine and sees a baby swimming in it -- proof of pregnancy, consequence of losing her virginity; an angry man at left, a laughing boy at right making an obscene gesture at the viewer. Provenance: Evert van Sypesteyn (until 1713) to Prince William IV (1733) to confiscated by the French to the Louvre 1795-1815 to Mauritshuis 1822. Do not confuse with Jan Steen's The Doctor's Visit (Mauritshuis inv. 168, c. 1660-70) -- a different painting by a different artist that dominates search results for this title. Dimensions: VERIFIED -- h 35 cm x w 28.6 cm (Google Arts and Culture / Mauritshuis data page). Medium: oil on panel.

### W15. Fire and Light (A Girl Places a Candle in a Lantern and a Boy Blows on the Coals in a Chafing-Dish) -- 1690-1706 -- Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (SK-A-370)

Attribution caveat: the Rijksmuseum lists it as "attributed to Godfried Schalcken", not as autograph. Oil on canvas; pendant of SK-A-371 (The Smoker). The two-element light system of the title -- candle-into-lantern plus boy blowing embers -- is the mature double-flame composition. Hofstede de Groot no. 237 gives canvas 18 x 15 in. (seen in search snippet only). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED (no dimensions on the Rijksmuseum page as rendered).

### W16. A Girl Blowing Out a Candle (Girl Blowing Out Taper; Hofstede de Groot no. 270, "A Girl Trying to Blow Out a Candle") -- date uncertain -- Alte Pinakothek, Munich (1904 cat. no. 434; in store-room since 1910)

Hofstede de Groot no. 270: a girl holds the candle and shades it with her left hand while another girl tries to blow it out; life-size, half-length; signed in full lower right; canvas 32 x 25 in.; formerly in the collection of Elector Palatine Johann Wilhelm, Dusseldorf. Date/dimension conflict flagged: an AI-generated FeelTheArt page dates it 1699-1706 and gives 64.8 x 80 cm; Hofstede de Groot gives no date and 32 x 25 in. (about 81.3 x 63.5 cm) -- the two dimension sets are roughly transposed and neither was verified on a museum page. Both are reported here and neither is verified. Dimensions: reported-but-unverified.

### W17. Portrait of William III, Prince of Orange, King of England and Stadtholder, by candlelight -- c. 1692-1697 -- Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (SK-A-367)

"A portrait of a ruler lit only by the flame of a candle. The candle has a symbolic meaning. It serves others with its light, while burning itself out. In other words, self-denial is a noble virtue." William in armour with an ermine cloak. Oil on canvas. The half-length portrait the biographical entry calls his best portrait example. Date conflict flagged: Rijksmuseum gives c. 1692-1697; the Franits thesis list of illustrations gives c. 1695-96. Dimensions: VERIFIED -- height 76.5 cm x width 65 cm (Rijksmuseum object page).

### W18. A Boy Blowing on a Firebrand to Light a Candle -- c. 1692-1698 -- National Galleries of Scotland, Edinburgh (NG 2495)

Oil on canvas; "likely that Schalcken executed this painting for Robert Spencer, 2nd Earl of Sunderland, while visiting Althorp during the period he spent in Britain". The Leiden Collection catalogue (GS-106) adds: painted spring 1692-autumn 1696; seen at Althorp by engraver George Vertue in the early 18th century; at least five old copies plus many 18th-century prints after it. The motif descends from El Greco's Boy Blowing on an Ember (c. 1570-75) and ultimately from Pliny's account of Antiphilus/Lycius. Date conflict flagged: NGS gives "About 1692-1698"; the Leiden catalogue narrows it to spring 1692-autumn 1696. Dimension note: Art Fund gives 75 x 64 cm; NGS (verified) gives 75.00 x 63.50 cm -- use the NGS figure. Dimensions: VERIFIED -- 75.00 x 63.50 cm (NGS object page; framed 104.00 x 92.00 x 10.00 cm).

### W19. Self-Portrait by Candlelight -- 1695 -- Galleria degli Uffizi, Florence (inv. no. 1878)

The nocturnal self-portrait Schalcken proposed himself for Cosimo III de' Medici's gallery of artists' self-portraits; painted during the London period 1692-96. Schalcken suggested his own inclusion through his London agent Thomas Platt; he proposed a nighttime setting because it was his foremost specialty and the gallery contained no other nocturnal self-portrait; he holds a mezzotint by John Smith (1693) after his now-lost Mary Magdalene by Candlelight; the highly finished drawing (Peck Collection, Ackland) dated 1694-95 likely functioned as a vidimus shown to the patron for approval; letters survive in the Archivio di Stato, Florence. A 1700 follow-up letter records his panic that a copy had been substituted in transit and his request for a sketch to confirm the pose. The Leiden Collection bio adds that he painted "at least two other candlelit self-portraits" in London. Dimensions: VERIFIED -- 92.3 x 81 cm, oil on canvas (Ackland Peck page, quoting the Uffizi; the Franits thesis list gives 92 x 81 cm, consistent).

### W20. Young Man Blowing a Torch to Light a Candle -- ca. 1692-96 -- The Leiden Collection, New York (GS-106; Godefridus Schalcken and Studio)

Oil on canvas; a young man half-length blows on burning wood from a brazier, sparks swirling, to light a candle in a brass sconce held in his right hand; an elegantly dressed woman (Fontange hairdo, pearl drop) gazes at the viewer from behind, incongruously placed in a kitchen/pantry (onions on the wall). Technical summary: single piece of medium-weight plain-weave canvas, lined; light ground; smooth opaque paint blended wet-into-wet, no impasto; shadows in thin glazes; flame/ember highlights in slightly raised brushwork; no underdrawing visible in infrared; unsigned and undated. Attribution caveat: catalogued as "Schalcken and Studio" -- the lighting effects are judged the master's touch, the figures stiffly painted by assistants. Modelled on W18 (Edinburgh), which the artist was evidently satisfied with. Provenance: Christie's Amsterdam, 16 Nov 2006, no. 70 (sold as "attributed to Schalcken"); on loan Nelson-Atkins 2009, Brooklyn Museum 2013-2016. Dimensions: VERIFIED -- 112.8 x 90 cm (Leiden Collection catalogue). The largest work in this set of 20, and the scale jump of the English period in one number.

## Etchings / prints

Autograph etching, verified: Portrait of Gerrit Dou Wearing a Beret; Bust, Full-Face to Left, Holding a Roll of Paper, ca. 1665-70, etching, 16.5 x 12.1 cm, The Leiden Collection (GS-130). Made during/just after his Leiden apprenticeship; inscribed "Honoris ergo / Praeceptorum Su'um delineavit / G. Schalcken" -- a tribute to his revered teacher. Hollstein 24:154, no. 4. Prints AFTER Schalcken (not by him), verified in sources read end to end: John Smith's mezzotint Mary Magdalene by Candlelight, 1693, 348 x 252 mm, British Museum (inv. 1855,0512.94), after Schalcken's now-lost painting -- commissioned by Schalcken shortly after his arrival in London. 18th-century mezzotints after W18 by Richard Purcell, Philip Corbut, Charles Spooner, Johan Faber the Younger and James Wilson. No other autograph etching title was verified end to end in this leg; his drawn oeuvre is small (fewer than forty sheets, per the Ackland page).

## Beyond the 20

Self-Portrait, 1679, oil on copper, Liechtenstein Museum, Vienna (marriage pendant: Portrait of Francoise van Diemen, his wife, 1679; dimensions 43 x 32 cm reported by a commercial site only -- NOT VERIFIED). Portrait of Sir Richard Levett, Lord Mayor of London, 1699 (English period; current location NOT VERIFIED). Self-Portrait, 1695, Leamington Spa Art Gallery and Museum, 109.5 x 88.5 cm (reported in the Franits thesis list of illustrations, snippet only -- NOT VERIFIED); another London-period candlelit self-portrait. Self-Portrait, 1706, wearing the gold chain from Johann Wilhelm (last self-portrait; current location NOT VERIFIED). Mary Stanhope, later Viscountess Fane, signed and dated 1702; prime version at Chevening, Kent. The Penitent Magdalen, dated 1700 per Pigage, canvas 37.5 x 27.5 in., formerly Elector Palatine collection, Germanic Museum Nuremberg 1893 cat. no. 342 (Hofstede de Groot no. 49, snippet only); and the Kassel Penitent Magdalen, canvas 16.5 x 13 in. (Hofstede de Groot no. 46, snippet only). A Girl Reading a Letter (A Girl with a Letter), Dresden -- reads a letter at a table by candlelight; Hofstede de Groot no. 269 gives 10 x 7.5 in. (snippet only). NOT VERIFIED. Woman Weaving a Crown of Flowers, 1675, National Gallery of Art, Washington (2005.26.1). Young Man with the Bust, 1672, Dresden (Gal.-Nr. 1789); Old Woman with a Book and Spectacles, Dresden (Gal.-Nr. 1788). Ceres Seeking Proserpine, Louvre; La Sainte Famille, Louvre (INV 1829, in storage). A Woman Singing and a Man with a Cittern, National Gallery, London (NG998); A Man Offering Gold and Coins to a Girl (A Candlelight Scene), National Gallery (NG999); Allegory of Virtue and Riches, 1667, National Gallery (NG199). The Smoker (A Man with a Pipe and a Man Pouring a Beverage), Rijksmuseum (SK-A-371), pendant to W15, attributed. Woman Selling Herrings, 1675-1680, Rijksmuseum (SK-A-2340); Young Woman with Lemon, 1685-1690, Rijksmuseum (SK-A-2339); Portrait of Josina Clara van Citters (1671-1753), 1699-1706, Rijksmuseum. The Doctor's Visit, signed and dated 1669, private collection, oil on panel 34 x 29 cm (per Sotheby's footnote, Beherman cat. no. 160); and The Doctor's Visit, late 1680s, sold Sotheby's New York 2019 (lot page read end to end; no dimensions stated for the lot itself). A Useless Moral Lesson (De nutteloze zedenles), Mauritshuis, pendant to W14. Girls with a Copybook, within a Stone Arch, 1668, Lamport Hall. A Woman Feeding Two Children by Candlelight, oil on canvas laid down on panel, 42 x 35.3 cm (Dorotheum 2024, lot; related version at Chatsworth, c. 1665-70 per Beherman -- snippet only).

## Evolution

Phase 1 -- Training and daylight (1656-c. 1675, Dordrecht/Leiden). Apprenticed to Samuel van Hoogstraten in Dordrecht at 13 (1656), then to Gerrit Dou in Leiden c. 1663-64. Early genre pictures "very closely resemble Dou's work". Crucially, the Leiden Collection catalogue (GS-101) states that daylight genre scenes -- old women reading, quack doctors, maidservants -- dominated his early output and he "only occasionally painted subjects with artificial illumination". W1-W5 fit: an Annunciation, an old woman scouring, an egg against a lamp, a scholar writing, a girl with a candle at a curtain -- small cabinet pictures in Dou's polished fijnschilder manner, mostly on panel/copper, mostly under about 40 cm.

Phase 2 -- Dordrecht maturity: the night takes over (c. 1675-1691). He "did not begin to paint many nocturnal scenes until the late 1670s" and "only after 1685 did the percentage of his work devoted to artificially lit scenes grow significantly, although night scenes were never so numerous as to represent the bulk of his oeuvre" (GS-101). The likely trigger was Dou's death in February 1675: Schalcken became "the only master able to meet the demand for top-quality depictions in this specific genre". At the same time he absorbed French classicism: a classical ideal of beauty, refined aristocratic figures, elevated biblical/classical subjects -- the candlelit girl is increasingly a beautiful, fashionable young woman rather than a kitchen maid (W10, W11). Portraiture became his economic engine: after Nicolaes Maes left for Amsterdam in 1673 he became Dordrecht's leading portrait painter; from the 1680s he travelled regularly to The Hague for commissions from William III's circle, joining the Pictura society in 1691. Portraiture "did not lend itself well to the time-consuming 'fine painting', and thus 'from time to time he turned to a more pleasing and airier manner of painting'" (Leiden bio) -- a loosening visible in the late portraits versus the enamel-smooth early panels. W6, W8, W9, W11-W16 belong here: game-playing, needle-threading, cherry-weighing, doctor-visiting, candle-blowing -- the moralising/erotic candlelit genre scene fully formed, still cabinet-scale (W11: 36 x 27.4 cm; W14: 35 x 28.6 cm).

Phase 3 -- England (1692-1697/98): scale and self-promotion. The candlelight specialty became a portrait device: "the diffuse candlelight allowed for a soft modeling of the face, giving the sitter an amiable and elegant impression" (Leiden bio). Works get dramatically larger -- W17 76.5 x 65 cm, W18 75 x 63.5 cm, W19 92.3 x 81 cm, W20 112.8 x 90 cm -- against the about 35 cm cabinet pictures of Phase 2. Subjects: the king in armour (W17), a virtuoso firebrand piece for the Earl of Sunderland (W18), and the self-portrait as international calling card for Cosimo III (W19), plus a studio-assisted large variant (W20). The English reception was mixed: Houbraken says he met with great success; Weyerman (hostile) says the English soon tired of portraits "as flat as unrisen pancakes" next to Kneller, and that he was "forced to paint history scenes and 'night lights'". The Leiden bio notes "very few portraits by Schalcken can be linked to his London years" -- the surviving English-period works are disproportionately the big candlelit showpieces, not the routine portraits. Period-end conflict flagged: Wikipedia gives England 1692-1697; the GS-106 endnotes infer the family left London at the end of 1696 (ninth child baptised in The Hague 3 February 1697); NGS dates W18 to c. 1692-1698.

Phase 4 -- Late The Hague / Dusseldorf (1698-1706). Back in June 1698, settled in The Hague; intensive contact with Elector Palatine Johann Wilhelm II, King Frederick IV of Denmark and King Frederick I of Prussia -- "one of the artist's most important patrons in the final phase of his life". Brief Dusseldorf sojourn 1703; Johann Wilhelm presented him with a gold chain, which he depicted in his self-portrait of 1706 -- his last self-portrait (Leiden bio). No work in the 20 securely dated to 1703-1706 was verified end to end in this leg; the late Magdalen dated 1700 (Munich, Hofstede de Groot no. 49) and the 1702 Mary Stanhope portrait (Chevening) are in Beyond the 20. Death-date conflict flagged: Wikipedia gives 16 November 1706; the Leiden Collection biography gives 13 November 1706, both in The Hague.

Scale summary (verified dimensions only): Phase 2 cabinet pictures about 35-36 cm on the long side (W11 36.0 x 27.4; W14 35 x 28.6); English-period canvases 65-113 cm (W17 76.5 x 65; W18 75 x 63.5; W19 92.3 x 81; W20 112.8 x 90). The light handling moves from a single small flame inside a dark interior (W3, W9) to multiple interacting light sources -- lantern + embers (W15), torch + brazier + candle (W20), sparks in the air -- and from daylight moralising (W2, W8) to night as the subject itself.

## Disputes and unverified items (flagged)

- C1. Birthplace: Made vs Dordrecht. Modern scholarship (Leiden Collection, Wikipedia, NGS, NGV, Wikimedia Commons) says Made (near Geertruidenberg), with the family moving to Dordrecht in 1654. The older tradition says Dordrecht: Bryan's Dictionary ("was born at Dordrecht in 1643"), Houbraken Translated ("was born in the year 1643 in Dordrecht, where his father was principle of the Latin school"), WahooArt ("Born in Dordrecht"), Meisterdrucke's fact table ("Born 1643 (Dordrecht)") -- though Meisterdrucke's own article text says "born in Made near Breda", contradicting its own table. Resolution: use Made (1643), and note the Dordrecht claim comes from Houbraken himself, who likely elided birthplace with the town where Schalcken grew up.
- C2. Death date: 16 November vs 13 November 1706. Wikipedia, Wikidata/Commons and Houbraken ("he also died on the 16th of November 1706", Houbraken Translated p. 176) say 16 November. The Leiden Collection (Piet Bakker, 2017) says 13 November. Both cannot be right; the 16th has Houbraken behind it, the 13th has the most recent scholarly biography behind it. Both are carried in the Site line; neither is presented as settled.
- C3. Exact birth date 8 October 1643. Only Wikipedia gives the day (8 October). Every other source read gives the year only. The day is unsourced in the pages read -- treat as Wikipedia-only.
- C4. Dou training dates and return to Dordrecht. Leiden Collection: to Dou "around 1663", back in Dordrecht "around 1669" (exact return "unknown"). Getty ULAN: returned to Dordrecht 1665 (standard-bearer with the arquebusiers). NGS: "In 1665 he returned to Dordrecht". Sotheby's: "almost certainly trained with Gerard Dou until 1665". Royal Collection snippet: "studied with Dou in Leiden during the years 1662-5". Wikipedia: "worked in Leiden until c. 1675, then returning to Dordrecht until 1691" -- the 1675 date contradicts all the others. Best-supported: Dou c. 1663-65, back in Dordrecht by 1665-69.
- C5. London years. Wikipedia 1692-1697; NGS "c. 1692-98"; Peck Collection "London period in 1692-96". Use 1692-1697 with the note that he was back in the Republic by June 1698.
- C6. Which William III is which. There are (at least) two candlelit William IIIs: the Rijksmuseum half-length, c. 1692-1697, 76.5 x 65 cm, SK-A-367; and the Mauritshuis full-length/seated portrait, 1699, inv. 158. The National Trust's Attingham Park version (King William III by Candlelight, 1690s, 62.2 x 74.9 cm) is a third version. Do not conflate them.
- C7. Wife's name spelling. Leiden Collection: "Francoisia van Diemen"; Getty ULAN: "Francoise van Dimen"; Wikipedia caption: "Francoise van Diemen". Same person (1661-1744, of Breda); spelling unsettled. The daughter is also Francoisia -- mother and daughter share the name, a trap for the reader.
- C8. Cosimo III's dates. Leiden Collection gives Cosimo III as (1642-1732); Peck Collection gives (1642-1723). Cosimo III died in 1723 -- the Leiden figure is an error. Do not copy it.
- C9. Lady at a Mirror by Candlelight dating. FeelTheArt (AI-generated, low authority) dates it 1699-1706; Hofstede de Groot lists it as no. 235 with 18th-century sale history but no firm date; the Mauritshuis page gives no date in the text read. Treat the date as uncertain, late career.
- C10. Toilet by Candle vs A Young Lady at Her Toilet. Wikipedia's "Toilet by Candle (The Hague)" and Hofstede no. 235 / Mauritshuis inv. 159 are the same picture under different titles.
- C11. Girl Blowing Out Taper identity. Wikipedia lists "Girl Blowing Out Taper (Munich)"; the Alte Pinakothek work in search results is titled A boy tries to blow out a girl's candle, dated 1699-1706, 64.8 x 80 cm; Hofstede no. 259 Teasing describes the same motif but as a small panel (7 x 5.5 in.). These may be versions of one composition or distinct works -- do not assert a single identity.
- C12. Name spelling. Godfried / Godefridus / Gottfried Schalcken / Schalken. Wikipedia's own URL is "Gottfried_Schalken" (redirect). ULAN's preferred form is "Schalcken, Godfried". Use Godfried Schalcken, note Godefridus as the Latinised form used by the Leiden Collection.
- C13. "Worked in Leiden until c. 1675" (Wikipedia). Contradicted by Leiden Collection, Getty, NGS and Sotheby's (all put the return to Dordrecht in the 1660s). Likely a Wikipedia error conflating the Leiden period with the Dordrecht period. Do not use.
- C14. Houbraken vs modern scholarship on birthplace and death date. Houbraken (the primary source, and Schalcken's own pupil) says Dordrecht 1643 and 16 November 1706; modern scholarship says Made 1643 and (Leiden Collection) 13 November 1706. The primary source is wrong on birthplace by modern evidence and may be wrong on the death day too -- or the modern 13th may be a slip. Both dates must be carried with their sources.
- C15. Family move to Dordrecht: "before he was four" vs 1654. Wikipedia says the family moved "before he was four years old"; the Leiden Collection (with archival footnotes) dates the move to 1654, when Cornelis became rector -- Schalcken would have been 10 or 11. The 1654 date is the sourced one; Wikipedia's "before four" is unsourced and inconsistent with the father's posting history.
- C16. "Fellow pupil of Aert de Gelder". Both were van Hoogstraten pupils, but the overlap is thin: Schalcken apprenticed 1656 and left for Dou c. 1663; de Gelder started under Hoogstraten "probably around 1660" (Sotheby's) and left for Rembrandt in Amsterdam (c. 1661-63), while Hoogstraten himself was in London 1662-67. They may have shared the Dordrecht workshop for about 1660-62 at most, and possibly not at all. State the shared teacher; do not assert a close fellow-pupil relationship.
- C17. "Employed by Johann Wilhelm in Dusseldorf" (Wikipedia) vs "brief sojourn" (Leiden Collection). Wikipedia's wording implies employment; Leiden says a brief 1703 visit during which the Elector gave him a gold chain. The gold chain is documented (depicted in the 1706 self-portrait); formal employment is not. Prefer "brief sojourn / honoured visit".

## Dimensions verified vs not verified (tally)

Verified (museum/collection page read end to end), 6 of the 20: W11 Young Lady Weighing Cherries 36.0 x 27.4 cm (NGV); W14 The Doctor's Examination h 35 x w 28.6 cm (Google Arts and Culture / Mauritshuis data); W17 William III 76.5 x 65 cm (Rijksmuseum); W18 Boy Blowing on a Firebrand 75.00 x 63.50 cm (NGS); W19 Self-Portrait by Candlelight 92.3 x 81 cm (Ackland Peck page, quoting the Uffizi); W20 Young Man Blowing a Torch 112.8 x 90 cm (Leiden Collection). Plus 1 etching verified: Portrait of Gerrit Dou 16.5 x 12.1 cm (Leiden Collection GS-130). Reported-but-unverified, 2 of the 20: W9 Girl Threading a Needle 7.5 x 6 in. (Hofstede de Groot snippet); W16 Girl Blowing Out a Candle 32 x 25 in. (Hofstede de Groot snippet; conflicting 64.8 x 80 cm from an AI-generated page). NOT VERIFIED, 12 of the 20: W1, W2, W3, W4, W5, W6, W7, W8, W10, W12, W13, W15.

## Aesthetic logic

Schalcken's pictures are rooms with one sun in them, and the sun is small, warm, and close. Everything in the frame -- face, hand, fabric, fruit, flame -- is placed by its distance from that one source, and the dark is not background but the material he sculpts with: the room the candle makes is the picture's real subject, and the candle is its only furniture. The finish is the opposite of de Gelder's: where de Gelder's paint stands up in ridges that catch real light, Schalcken's is enamel-smooth, thin, patient, fused -- the labour hidden so the light can look effortless. That is why his portraits flattered: a soft, diffuse, near light forgives a face, and the rich paid for forgiveness. It is also why the English, trained on Kneller's round and loose modelling, called the same pictures flat. The mechanism has a built-in limit Houbraken names outright: Schalcken is Van der Werff's equal in brush, colour-mixing, nudes and fabric, but sits at his "foot stool" for drawing -- which is why his best pictures are small, close, and organised by a single light rather than by complex figure composition. The candle is both the picture's light and its argument: a flame that serves others while burning itself out, which is why a king could be shown by it at all. And the flame is fragile -- a boy's breath away from going out -- so the picture always carries its own ending inside it: the light that makes it is the thing it is about to lose.

## Positions

- P1. Schalcken's real subject is not the candle but the room the candle makes: a small, warm, bounded world in which one flame decides what exists and what disappears. The dark is not background; it is the material he sculpts with.
- P2. His career is a negotiation between slowness and money. The fine finish that made his name was too slow for portrait volume, so he loosened it ("a more pleasing and airier manner") for paying sitters while keeping the candlelight itself as the brand. The light, not the finish, is the constant.
- P3. Houbraken's de Piles comparison (Q4) is the sharpest contemporary criticism: Schalcken is Van der Werff's equal in brush, colour-mixing, nudes and fabric, but sits at his "foot stool" for drawing. His weakness, by his own biographer's account, was construction -- which is why his best pictures are small, close, and organised by a single light rather than by complex figure composition. Houbraken notes the Denial of St Peter approvingly precisely because there "the figures were firmly drawn and every part had its proper relationship to the whole, a matter in which he otherwise sometimes erred."
- P4. The Uffizi self-portrait episode (M8) shows Schalcken as a deliberate self-promoter: he asked to be included, chose the one setting no other portrait in the gallery had (night), and tailored the prop (a print, for a print collector) to the patron. The candlelight specialism was a marketing position as much as a technique.
- P5. The Weyerman hostility (Q7) and the Houbraken praise (Q1) are the same observation from opposite sides: soft, diffuse, flattering light reads as elegance to a Dutch audience and as flatness to an English one trained on Kneller's round, loose modelling. Schalcken's London "failure" is partly a clash of national portrait tastes, not a collapse of patronage -- Houbraken says he "amassed a lot of money" there.
- P6. The peephole anecdote (Q9), whether true or not, captures the right idea about his method: the light must be fixed before it can be painted. A flickering flame cannot be transcribed from life; it must be frozen -- by a peephole, a dark room, or sheer slow looking -- and then rebuilt in thin, patient layers. For a generative piece, the lesson is: lock the light first, then let everything else move around it.

## Quotes

No quotes BY Schalcken were found in any source read end to end. He left no treatise, letters, or recorded sayings in the material consulted; the surviving letters about the Uffizi commission are between his agent Thomas Platt and Cosimo's secretary Bassetti, not by Schalcken himself. All quotes below are ABOUT him.

- Q1 (about him -- Houbraken, via Houbraken Translated, vol. 3, p. 177): "He especially owed his fame to his candlelight scenes, which he was able to paint so naturally and powerfully that I do not know if anyone was his equal in them."
- Q2 (about him -- Houbraken, via Houbraken Translated, vol. 3, p. 176): "It is said that he worked a month on the carpet hanging." [On Lady, Come into the Garden.]
- Q3 (about him -- Houbraken, via Houbraken Translated, vol. 3, p. 177): "Also, he often had his figures loom up through candle- or daylight, or lit by the sun through a cloth, so that the flesh might reveal itself more pleasantly through the clear reflection, which he was able to do so skilfully that it flattered and charmed the eyes of everyone."
- Q4 (about him -- Houbraken, via Houbraken Translated, vol. 3, p. 177): "If we were to follow the example of Roger de Piles and compare one artist to another, we would find reason to place Schalcken with the knight Van der Werff with respect to his flattering brush, the artful mixing of his colours, his nudes and natural imitation of velvet and other fabrics, but with respect to his drawing I would assign him his foot stool."
- Q5 (about him -- Houbraken, via Houbraken Translated, vol. 3, p. 176): "He was one of the happiest of Dutch painters, seeing that the art of his brush was amply paid from the beginning to the end of his life, so that he reaped the rewards of his labours while alive, which occurs but very rarely."
- Q6 (about him -- Houbraken, quoted in the Leiden Collection biography): Schalcken was influenced primarily by Dou, "whose facture he was able to emulate almost perfectly."
- Q7 (about him -- Weyerman, quoted in the Leiden Collection biography): the English soon had enough of Schalcken's portraits, which were "as flat as unrisen pancakes," lacking "the firm, round, loose, and powerful manner that [viewers] discerned in the delightful likenesses by the Knight [Godfried] Kneller [...] and many others in that century of blossoming portraitists."
- Q8 (about him -- Bryan's Dictionary, p. 82): "The chief merit of Schalcken consists in the neatness of his finishing, and the perfect intelligence of his chiaro-scuro. His touch is mellow, but too fused, and his colour warm and golden."
- Q9 (about him -- Bryan's Dictionary, p. 82; tradition, hedged in the original): "To give the most natural effect to his candle-light pieces, he is said to have adopted the following system: he placed the object he intended to paint in a dark room, with a candle, and looking through a small hole, painted by day what he saw by candle-light."
- Q10 (about him -- Hofstede de Groot, catalogue raisonne vol. 5, p. 326): "He tried also to combine the effects of two sources of light, such as the moon and a candle, or a ray of sunlight entering a room and a charcoal fire. These experiments were very highly admired by his contemporaries and by the whole eighteenth century, but they have lost their attraction now."
- Q11 (about him -- Rijksmuseum, SK-A-367, on the William III): "Een opvallend schilderij: een portret van een heerser, slechts verlicht door een kaars. De kaars heeft een symbolische betekenis. Met zijn licht dient hij anderen, terwijl hij zelf opbrandt." [A striking painting: a portrait of a ruler, lit only by a candle. The candle has a symbolic meaning. With its light it serves others, while it burns itself up.]
- Q12 (about him -- Houbraken, via Houbraken Translated, vol. 3, p. 177, on the lost Denial of St Peter): "The boldness of the maid servant, who shines a candle into his eyes, and the hesitancy and shyness of Peter, could clearly be discerned in the facial features."

## Network

Schalcken's network is a candlelit line, not a court one -- until the end, when the court comes to the candle (leg 3 counted 59 unique persons). Teachers: 2 -- Samuel van Hoogstraten (1627-78); Gerrit Dou (1613-75). Fellow pupil under van Hoogstraten: 1 -- Aert de Gelder (1645-1727), the #547 subject; the shared bench is thin (see C16). Fellow pupils under Dou: 10 -- Frans van Mieris the Elder (1635-81), Gabriel Metsu (1629-67), Carel de Moor (1656-1738), Pieter Cornelisz van Slingelandt (1640-91), Matthijs Naiveu (1647-1726), Abraham de Pape (d. 1666), Domenicus van Tol (c. 1635-76), Bartholomeus Maton (1641-after 1682), Gijsbert Andriesz Verbrugge (no dates found), Pieter Hermansz Verelst (1618-78). Family: 9 -- Cornelis Schalcken (1610-74), father; Aletta Lydius (1612-78), mother; Francoisia van Diemen (1661-1744), wife; Christoffel van Diemen (d. 1661), father-in-law; Elisabeth Beens (d. 1661), mother-in-law; Francoisia Schalcken (b. 1690), daughter, the only child to reach adulthood; Pieter Roman (1676-after 1733), son-in-law; Maria Schalcken (c. 1645/49 - before 1699), sister and pupil; Jacob Schalcken (cousin or nephew, no dates found), pupil. Pupils of his own (non-family): 8 -- Arnold Boonen (1669-1729), Godefridus Callenfels (no dates found), Simon Germain (1650-1719), Richard Morris (no dates found), Arent Pijl (c. 1680-1721), Anthony Vreem (1660-81), Justus van Bentum (1670-1727), Giamagli (surname only). Pupil of a pupil: 1 -- Philip van Dyck (1680-1753), Boonen's pupil. Third-generation fijnschilder peers (not already counted): 2 -- Eglon van der Neer (1635/36-1703), Caspar Netscher (1639-84). Patrons: 7 -- William III (1650-1702), Cosimo III de' Medici (1642-1723), Johann Wilhelm II, Elector Palatine (1658-1716), Anna Maria Luisa de' Medici (1667-1743), Frederick IV of Denmark (1671-1730), Frederick I of Prussia (1657-1713), Pieter de la Court (1618-85). Sitters (named): 5 -- Sir Richard Levett (1644-1711), John Acton (no dates found), Margaret Acton, nee Cutts (no dates found), Diederick Hoeufft (1648-1719), Josina Parduyn (1642-1718). Colleagues and rivals: 4 -- Godfrey Kneller (1646-1723), Adriaen van der Werff (1659-1722), Jan Frans van Douven (1656-1727), John Smith (1652-1743), engraver. Root of the line: 1 -- Rembrandt van Rijn (1606-69), teacher of both van Hoogstraten and Dou. Sources, writers and scholars: 9 -- Arnold Houbraken (1660-1719), Jan van Gool (1685-1763), Jacob Campo Weyerman (1677-1747), Thierry Beherman (20th c.), Wayne Franits (dates not established), Piet Bakker (dates not established), Cornelis Hofstede de Groot (1863-1930), John Smith (1781-1855), catalogue author, Sheridan Le Fanu (1814-73). Dual roles counted once each: Carel de Moor (fellow Dou pupil + Schalcken pupil); Maria Schalcken and Jacob Schalcken (family + pupils).

The shape of it: a minister's son from Made, moved to Dordrecht at ten, apprenticed at thirteen to the Rembrandt pupil in town, then to Rembrandt's first pupil in Leiden -- two steps from Rembrandt on both sides -- and from there a career built on a single trick done better than anyone: one candle in a dark room. His sister learned it beside him and may have painted some of the pictures that still carry his name; his nephew painted so like him the attributions still blur; his pupils carried the candle to the German courts (Boonen) and one remove down the line (van Dyck, through Boonen). His patrons are the hinge of the next study: William III, who sat for him by candlelight; Cosimo III, who wanted his face for the Uffizi at night; and Johann Wilhelm, who gave him a gold chain in the same year he knighted Adriaen van der Werff -- the shared patron who ties the candlelight painter to the porcelain painter. The writers who fixed him are his own pupil Houbraken (the primary source, and the #546 subject), the hostile Weyerman (the pancakes), and the modern scholars Bakker, Franits and Beherman. Rembrandt himself is verified never studied (zero study headers; 9 incidental mentions, no local file) but is NOT proposed: he is the root of the whole line, not the next hop, and a Rembrandt study would be a different kind of undertaking. The same applies in miniature to Vermeer (carried in the #522-#529 block, held at the parent's discretion).

## Future candidates
Top pick for #549 is Adriaen van der Werff (21 January 1659 - 12 November 1722) -- the carried main, promoted -- and the promotion is stronger now than the #547 file left it. #547 called Werff "the verdict pick" whose "link to de Gelder is contrast only, no personal link at all". Against Schalcken the link is personal and double, and it is documented in this leg: (1) Shared patron, same court, same years. Johann Wilhelm, Elector Palatine, visited Werff in Rotterdam in 1696, put him on a princely salary, and knighted him and made him official court painter in 1703 -- the same year Schalcken was in Dusseldorf in the Elector's service and received the gold chain he wears in his 1706 self-portrait. Both men were honoured by the same prince in the same year, for the same kind of service. (2) Shared Medici thread. Both sent self-portraits to Cosimo III de' Medici in Florence: Werff's was ordered by Johann Wilhelm and Anna Maria Luisa for Cosimo's gallery of self-portraits, and Schalcken sent his nocturnal self-portrait to Cosimo around 1695 at Cosimo's request. Cosimo had visited Dou's studio in 1669 -- the Medici link runs through Schalcken's teacher as well. (3) The fijnschilder succession. Werff was Eglon van der Neer's pupil; van der Neer was Schalcken's fellow third-generation fijnschilder and himself a Johann Wilhelm court painter (1696-98). The chain is: Dou to Schalcken and van der Neer to Werff, with all three serving the same Elector. (4) The contrast is still the mechanism. Arnold Houbraken called Werff "the greatest of the Dutch painters"; his enamel-smooth "porcelain finish" is the antithesis of de Gelder's impasto -- and the refinement of Schalcken's candlelight polish, taken to its court extreme. The piece-prompts ground writes itself: one light source, polished to porcelain, versus one light source, left rough. Against the other carried mains, stated plainly: Nicolaes Maes is the Dordrecht Rembrandt pupil who invented the eavesdropper, but he left Dordrecht for Amsterdam in 1673 -- after which Schalcken became Dordrecht's leading portrait painter, so the link is succession, not contact. Frans van Mieris the Elder is the style-source (Schalcken assimilated his lightness), but he died in 1681 and the link is influence, not a shared bench, court or patron. Werff has the bench (via van der Neer), the court (Dusseldorf), the patron (Johann Wilhelm), the year (1703), and the Medici thread -- four independent links against one each for the others. The doorway is the shared patron. (The 14 names carried in #547 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Govert Flinck (25 January 1615 - 2 February 1660), Ferdinand Bol (24 June 1616 - 24 August 1680), Willem Drost (baptised 19 April 1633 - buried 25 February 1659) (mains); Jacob Moelaert (1649-1727) (reserve); Margaretha van Godewijk (30 or 31 August 1627 - 2 November 1677), Johannes Vollevens (1649, Geertruidenberg - 1728, The Hague) (alternates); spares: Matthijs Balen (1684-1766), Hendrik Noteman (1654-1737), Augustin Terwesten (1649-1711), Werner Sumowski (8 March 1931 - 3 September 2015), Ernst van de Wetering (9 March 1938 - 11 August 2021), Jacob Adriaensz Backer (1608/9 - 27 August 1651), Barthoud van Slingelandt (1654-1711), Godfrey Kneller (8 August 1646 - 19 October 1723). Govert Flinck (25 January 1615 - 2 February 1660; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 135 lines) -- main 1, strongest NEW name: the Rembrandt pupil who left the manner for Rubens, and whose last commission Rembrandt himself inherited. Apprenticed to a silk mercer, then Lambert Jacobsz in Leeuwarden with Jacob Backer; Sandrart, visiting Holland in 1637, found him acknowledged as one of Rembrandt's best pupils, living in the dealer Hendrick van Uylenburgh's house; he followed Rembrandt's style in all works 1636-1648, then turned to the swelling forms and grand action of Rubens, winning the patronage of Friedrich Wilhelm I, the Great Elector of Brandenburg, and John Maurice of Nassau; Amsterdam burgher 1652, married the heiress Sophie van der Houven 1656, intimate with the poet Joost van den Vondel; in November 1659 the burgomaster contracted him for 12 canvases of David, Samson, Manius Curius Dentatus, Horatius Cocles and the Batavians, and he died leaving only sketches -- Rembrandt was asked to fill one and produced The Conspiracy of Claudius Civilis, which the authorities rejected. His Landscape with Obelisk was historically mistaken for Rembrandt and was stolen in the 1990 Gardner heist. The doorway is the Rembrandt pupil who left the manner for Rubens -- and whose last commission Rembrandt inherited. Ferdinand Bol (24 June 1616 - 24 August 1680; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 93 lines) -- main 2: the Dordrecht Rembrandt pupil who became Amsterdam's most commissioned painter, and whose pictures were mistaken for Rembrandt's for a century. Born in Dordrecht, son of the surgeon Balthasar Bol (painted by Rembrandt twenty years later); apprentice first of Jacob Gerritsz Cuyp in Dordrecht and/or Abraham Bloemaert in Utrecht, then of Rembrandt after 1630, living in his house on the Sint Antoniesbreestraat, neighbours with Flinck; own studio 1641; Amsterdam burgher 1652; married Elisabeth Dell 1653, whose father held Admiralty and wine-merchants' posts that later commissioned him; head of the guild 1655, chimney pieces for Jacob van Campen's new town hall and four more for the Admiralty; by the mid-1650s receiving more official commissions than any other artist in Amsterdam, his palette lightened and his figures grown elegant; four paintings for the Trip brothers' mansions; his pupil was Godfrey Kneller; he retired from painting after remarrying Anna van Erckel in 1669 and died on the Herengracht. His Portrait of Elisabeth Bas and many others were falsely attributed to Rembrandt in the 19th century. The doorway is the Dordrecht boy who out-commissioned every painter in Amsterdam -- and was then mistaken for his master. Willem Drost (baptised 19 April 1633 - buried 25 February 1659; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 163 lines) -- main 3: the mysterious pupil who died at 25 in Venice, and whose pictures still carry Rembrandt's name. Rembrandt's student in the late 1640s or early 1650s per Houbraken, developing a close working relationship; his 1654 Bathsheba with King David's Letter answers Rembrandt's Bathsheba of the same year, same subject, different treatment, both now in the Louvre; Amsterdam until 1655, then Rome, friend of Karel Lot (Johann Carl Loth) and Joan vander Meer, collaborating with Loth on a lost Four Evangelists series in Venice, where he died; his Portrait of a Young Woman with her Hands Folded on a Book was attributed to Rembrandt for more than 300 years, and the Polish Rider -- found 1897, Frick Collection -- has been argued as his since Josua Bruyn in 1984, though the Frick has not changed the attribution, and a group of "Rembrandt" drawings reattributed to Drost is more widely accepted. The doorway is the pupil who died at 25 in Venice and left Rembrandt holding his name. Jacob Moelaert (1649-1727; dates from the Leiden Collection de Gelder biography snippet) -- the reserve: the friend who left de Gelder his Rembrandts. Amateur painter and printseller, pupil of Nicolaes Maes in Dordrecht; well acquainted with de Gelder, and at his death in 1727 left de Gelder three albums of prints and drawings, including one with work by Rembrandt; de Gelder died suddenly less than a month later, on 27 August of that same year. He is the reserve because his painted oeuvre is thin -- amateur and dealer first -- though his mechanism (the print album as a bequest, the collection as a friendship) is the most intimate in the network. The doorway is the friend whose albums outlived them both by a month. Margaretha van Godewijk (30 or 31 August 1627, Dordrecht - 2 November 1677, Dordrecht; dates search-verified only) -- alternate 1: the Dordrecht polymath with no surviving works. Poet and painter; her father, a Latin-school teacher, taught her Greek, Latin, Italian, French and English; learned to paint from Nicolaes Maes per Houbraken (the RKD says pupil of Cornelis Bisschop), skilled at landscapes, villas, houses, flowers and ships in oils, watercolours and embroidery, good at astronomy and at diamond-engraving on glass roemers; Houbraken quoted the Dordrecht historian Mathias Balen (1611-1691) on her among his famous women. She is alternate 1 because no works survive -- the study would be built from the life and the loss -- but the doorway (a woman who painted, wrote, and engraved glass in a town that kept Rembrandt's manner) is the sharpest in the alternates. The doorway is the Dordrecht woman who painted, wrote poetry, and engraved glass -- and left nothing to look at. Johannes Vollevens (1649, Geertruidenberg - 1728, The Hague; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 25 lines) -- alternate 2: the portraitist whose birth year Houbraken got wrong by five years. Born in Geertruidenberg, sketched and drew in charcoal so enthusiastically his parents sent him to learn drawing; first lessons from Caspar Netscher per Houbraken, then Nicolaes Maes, then Jan de Baen in The Hague (RKD: Maes's pupil in Dordrecht, moved to The Hague 1664, studied with de Baen); joined the Confrerie Pictura 1674; his son Johannes Vollevens II also became a respected portrait painter; his portrait of Gaspar Fagel survives. He is alternate 2 because his mechanism (the provincial portraitist who kept moving to the next master) is a career shape rather than a system -- but the Houbraken error (born 1654, "the same year that took Fabritius by a stroke of fate", in fact five years earlier) is the chain's own correction made visible. The doorway is the painter Houbraken misdated by the Delft explosion. Werner Sumowski (8 March 1931, Ortelsburg - 3 September 2015, Stuttgart; dates from the HNA obituary snippet) -- spare 4: the scholar who knew the Rembrandt school better than anyone alive. Professor at Stuttgart 1973-1993; from 1979 his ten-volume opus on the drawings of Rembrandt's pupils, and six volumes on the paintings (1983, with later additions) covering 99 named artists and more than 2,500 works; Gary Schwartz's verdict: he knew "more about the Rembrandt school as anyone ever alive". He is a spare, not a main, because he is the cataloguer of the line, not a maker in it -- but the line's attribution problem (de Gelder, Drost, Bol all carried Rembrandt's name) is his life's work. The doorway is the man who sorted ninety-nine pupils from their master. Ernst van de Wetering (9 March 1938 - 11 August 2021; dates from Dictionary of Art Historians and EN Wikipedia snippets) -- spare 5: the Rembrandt police. Joined the Rembrandt Research Project in 1968, chairman from 1993, directed its later stages; doctorate cum laude 1986 on the workshop practice of the early Rembrandt; professor at Amsterdam 1987-1999, Slade Professor at Oxford 2002-03; Rembrandt: the Painter at Work (1997) and Rembrandt: The Painter Thinking (2016); the six-volume A Corpus of Rembrandt Paintings (1982-2014) was the project's principal result; his evaluations were so conclusive on value that he was dubbed "the Rembrandt police", and Gary Schwartz called his work "deleterious to scholarship" because it ended debate on authenticity. The doorway is the chairman who decided which Rembrandts were Rembrandts. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate. Jacob Adriaensz Backer (1608/9, Harlingen - 27 August 1651, Amsterdam; dates from Leiden Collection and EN Wikipedia snippets) -- spare 6: Flinck's bedfellow, and the pupil who may never have been a pupil. Mennonite baker's son; with Flinck a pupil of Lambert Jacobsz in Leeuwarden 1627-1633 -- Houbraken calls Backer Flinck's "bedfellow and companion in Art" -- then Amsterdam, probably working for Hendrick van Uylenburgh; about 140 paintings in twenty years, portraits, religious and mythological subjects, his Company of Cornelis de Graeff schutterstuk hung for years on the same wall as the Night Watch; taught Abraham van den Tempel; died at 42, three months after joining the Remonstrant congregation. The doorway is Flinck's companion, whose Rembrandt apprenticeship may be a three-hundred-year misreading. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions ("Jacob Adriaensz Backer" 0; "Jacob Backer" 0); no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate. FLAG: birth 1608 vs 1608/9 vs 1609 (Alchetron); Rembrandt-pupil status disputed -- Leiden Collection: his Amsterdam move "was not related to a period of study with Rembrandt", the claim resting on a misreading of Houbraken; Wikipedia: "joined Rembrandt's studio between 1632 and 1634". Barthoud van Slingelandt (1654-1711; dates from the Leiden Collection de Gelder biography snippet) -- spare 7: the burgomaster who gave Terwesten a round room. Burgomaster of Dordrecht; in his house Terwesten was painting "een kamer in het rond" when de Gelder and Noteman visited. He is a spare because he is a patron, not a maker -- but he is the only named Dordrecht patron in de Gelder's circle, and the round room is the commission the visit turned on. The doorway is the burgomaster whose walls went round. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate. FLAG: dates rest on the Leiden Collection snippet only. Godfrey Kneller (8 August 1646, Lubeck - 19 October 1723, London; dates from EN Wikipedia snippet) -- spare 8: Bol's pupil who became England's court painter. Born Gottfried Kniller, son of the Lubeck portrait painter Zacharias Kniller; studied mathematics at Leiden, then became a pupil of Ferdinand Bol and Rembrandt in Amsterdam by his father's choice; earliest dated work, Johann Philipp von Schonborn, 1666; with his brother John Zacharias Kneller to Rome and Venice in the early 1670s, in the studio of Carlo Maratti; to England 1676, patronage of the Duke of Monmouth; the face of Restoration and Augustan England for the next four decades. The doorway is the Lubeck boy Bol taught, who painted every face in England. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Christian Tumpel (31 March 1937, Bielefeld - 9 September 2009, Bad Kissingen). German art historian active in the Netherlands; doctorate at Hamburg on Rembrandt, Warburg Institute 1968-69, professor at Nijmegen 1984-2002; with his wife Astrid Tumpel, the catalogue raisonne on Rembrandt (1986) and Rembrandt: Images and Metaphors (2006). Held back because he is a scholar, not a maker, and his ground overlaps Sumowski and van de Wetering. Gary Schwartz (b. 1940). Rembrandt scholar, publisher and columnist; Kress fellow, then English-language secretary to Prof. J. G. van Gelder (1903-80) at Utrecht; his Rembrandt: His Life, His Paintings (1968, with Horst Gerson's text) caused a stir by cutting Bredius's 610 to 380; fifty years in the authenticity debates, calling van de Wetering's work "deleterious to scholarship". Held back because he is a living scholar, not a maker. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#546. Rembrandt's Leiden studio-sharer, c. 1626-1631, fellow pupil of Pieter Lastman; they split in 1631, Rembrandt to Amsterdam, Lievens to England, then Antwerp 1635-1653 under Rubens and Van Dyck; in 1656 Rembrandt still owned paintings by his former friend. Held back because he is a big figure who needs a full study slot of its own -- the same note the Lastman carried entry gives him. (The 12 names carried in #546 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Adriaen van der Werff (21 January 1659 - 12 November 1722), Nicolaes Maes (January 1634 - December 1693) (mains); Frans van Mieris the Elder (16 April 1635 - 12 March 1681), Caspar Netscher (c. 1639 - 15 January 1684) (alternates); spares: Arnold Boonen (16 December 1669 - 2 October 1729), Eglon Hendrik van der Neer (1635/36 - 3 May 1703), Antonina Houbraken (30 May 1686 - December 1736), David van Hoogstraten (14 March 1658 - 21 November 1724), Giorgio Vasari (30 July 1511 - 27 June 1574), Cornelis de Bie (10 February 1627 - c. 1712-1715), Filippo Baldinucci (3 June 1625 - 10 January 1696), Willem van Drielenburch (1632 - c. 1687). Adriaen van der Werff (21 January 1659 - 12 November 1722; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 126 lines) - main 2: the polished opposite of de Gelder, and the painter Arnold himself ranked highest: "Arnold Houbraken, writing in 1718, considered him the greatest of the Dutch painters". Pupil of Eglon van der Neer (1671-1676); Rotterdam master at 17; head of the Rotterdam Guild of St Luke 1691 and 1695; court painter to Johann Wilhelm, Elector Palatine, from 1696/97, knighted 1703; his enamel-smooth "porcelain finish" carried the Leiden fijnschilder manner onto large history pictures for the Dusseldorf court, his 34 paintings hung in a room next to Rembrandt's; reputation collapsed in the 19th century as a betrayer of the Dutch naturalistic tradition. He is main 2 rather than top pick because his link to Arnold is a verdict, not a shared workshop: Arnold crowned him, where de Gelder is Arnold's teacher-brother and townsman. The doorway is the painter Arnold called the greatest of the Dutch painters -- the porcelain finish against de Gelder's impasto. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions ("Adriaen van der Werff" 0; "Werff" 0); no local file; muse repo study/adriaen-van-der-werff.md 404; not carried in #522-#545 as a candidate. FLAG: EN Wikipedia says both "In December 1704, he painted Prince John Churchill" and lists the Marlborough portrait as "(12 December 1701)"; "His only daughter married to Flemish painter Adriaen Brouwer" is impossible (Brouwer died 1638) and is not used here; Eglon tutelage 1671-1676 (Werff article) vs 1671-1675 (Leiden Collection). Nicolaes Maes (January 1634 - December 1693, buried 24 December 1693; dates search-verified only) - main 3: the Dordrecht Rembrandt pupil who broke away and invented the eavesdropper. In Rembrandt's Amsterdam studio c. 1648-1653, back in Dordrecht by December 1653 for twenty years, then Amsterdam from 1673, where he painted some 900 portraits and died rich. His Dordrecht genre pictures (1655-1665) are the hinge of the chain: the eavesdropper at the foot of the stairs, finger to her lips, breaking the fourth wall -- a domestic interior as a listening machine, the exact subject Vermeer would inherit. His registered Dordrecht pupils included Justus de Gelder, Margaretha van Godewijk, Jacob Moelaert and Johannes Vollevens. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he left Dordrecht for Amsterdam portraiture, where de Gelder stayed and kept Rembrandt's manner alive in the town itself. The doorway is the Dordrecht pupil who left Rembrandt, invented the eavesdropper, and then painted nine hundred portraits. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions ("Nicolaes Maes" 0); no local file; muse repo study/nicolaes-maes.md 404; not carried in #522-#545 as a candidate (mentioned once inside a carried entry, as the man Jacob's recommendation "is said to have sent... to Rembrandt" -- a mention, not a proposal). FLAG: death 24 December 1693 (burial, Wikipedia) vs 24 November 1693 (Prabook, Alchetron). Frans van Mieris the Elder (16 April 1635 - 12 March 1681; dates search-verified only) - alternate 1: the Leiden fine painter at his smallest and most polished. Goldsmith's son; glass-painter's pupil, then Gerard Dou's "Prince of my students"; panels seldom larger than 12-15 inches; shiny brightness and metallic polish, satin skirts, oyster luncheons and doctor's visits; founder of a three-generation Leiden dynasty (Willem, Frans the Younger). Houbraken is the source for his birth date, and the 1911 Britannica already doubts it. He is alternate 1 because his mechanism (the miniature polish) overlaps van der Werff's, and his life is short on incident -- but he is the fijnschilder standard against which Werff and Schalcken are measured. The doorway is the prince of Dou's students, painting satin on panels the size of a hand. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions ("Frans van Mieris" 0); no local file; muse repo study/frans-van-mieris-the-elder.md 404 (and study/frans-van-mieris.md 404); not carried in #522-#545 as a candidate (mentioned once inside a carried entry, as a follower's model -- a mention, not a proposal). FLAG: birth 16 April 1635 "according to Houbraken" (1911 Britannica: "It is a question whether Houbraken has truly recorded this master's birthday" -- an oyster-luncheon picture is dated 1650); "? 1635" in the 1911 Britannica heading. Caspar Netscher (c. 1639 - 15 January 1684; dates search-verified only) - alternate 2: the Hague portraitist who painted silk. Orphaned young, raised in Arnhem, ter Borch's most gifted pupil in Deventer (c. 1655-1659), then The Hague from 1662, where from the 1670s he was the most sought-after portrait painter in the city; master of oriental rugs, silk and brocade, "introduced an international style to the Northern Netherlands"; painted lying in bed with gout until 15 January 1684, when, "according to Houbraken, 'this affliction affected his bowls, and caused his death'". He is alternate 2 because his mechanism (fabric as the picture) is a single brilliant trick rather than a system -- but it is the trick the whole Hague portrait trade ran on. The doorway is the painter who sold silk by the yard and died painting in bed. FLAG: birth c. 1636 (Rijksmuseum, after Wieseman) vs c. 1639 (Houbraken, Wikipedia); birthplace Heidelberg vs Prague; father a sculptor (Houbraken) vs a painter (Wieseman). Arnold Boonen (16 December 1669 - 2 October 1729; dates search-verified only) - spare 1: the Dordrecht portraitist who carried the candle out of the genre piece and into the state portrait. Pupil first of Arnold Verbuis, then six years (from 1683) under Schalcken; small candlelit genre pictures before 1700, then life-size portraits at the German courts (1694-95) and in Amsterdam from 1696 -- Peter the Great, Marlborough, the Prince and Princess of Orange; teacher of Cornelis Troost. The doorway is the Schalcken pupil who took candlelight to the German courts and came home to paint Peter the Great.
