FLAG: an "Anatomy Lesson" dated 1731 circulates under his name, two years after his death (misattribution or another hand). Eglon Hendrik van der Neer (1635/36 - 3 May 1703; dates search-verified only) - spare 2: the teacher between the generations: son of the moonlit landscape painter Aert van der Neer, pupil of Jacob van Loo, friend of Frans van Mieris the Elder (probably known since 1658), and the man who taught Adriaen van der Werff in Rotterdam (1671-1675/76) -- "Houbraken mentions only one pupil, Adriaen van der Werff". Court painter to Charles II of Spain (1687) and to the Elector Palatine in Dusseldorf (1690/1698); married the artist Adriana Spilberg 1697; died in Dusseldorf 1703. Elegant fashionable people, then landscapes; the figure-painter who put the figures into Jan van der Heyden's town views. The doorway is the teacher of the greatest Dutch painter -- the link between Mieris and van der Werff. FLAG: birth c. 1634 (Invaluable, from Houbraken's "died aged seventy") vs 1635/36 (Wikipedia, Schavemaker) vs "around 1635" (Leiden Collection); Werff tutelage 1671-1675 (Leiden) vs 1671-1676 (Werff article). Antonina Houbraken (30 May 1686 - December 1736, buried 12 December 1736; dates search-verified only) - spare 3: PROMOTED from the #545 extra verified spares, where she was held back only because no dates were established; this run establishes them (born 30 May 1686, baptised 31 May 1686 -- see C569). The daughter who drew the country. Draughtswoman and portraitist; learned in her father's workshop; assisted father and brother on the Schouburgh designs and provided designs for Pieter Tanje; married the draughtsman Jacobus Stellingwerff 1723 and, after his death in 1727, kept supplying topographical drawings to the collectors Andries and Gerrit Schoemaker until at least 1729 -- hundreds of Dutch sites, towns and buildings drawn on the spot, later embellished with animals and people. The doorway is the daughter who drew the whole country for the collectors' albums. FLAG: death given as "December 1736" (EN Wikipedia) with burial 12 December 1736; Prabook's "March 31, 1686" is wrong (see C569). David van Hoogstraten (14 March 1658 - 21 November 1724; dates search-verified only) - spare 4: the poet inside the painter's book. Physician, poet, philologist; Samuel van Hoogstraten's nephew (son of Samuel's brother Frans); prorector of the Amsterdam Latin School; his annotated Latin Phaedrus edition (1701) is his masterpiece, his Dutch verse Phaedrus (1703) a bestseller; his 30-line laudatory poem opens Schouburgh vol. 1; his Groot woordenboek (1725-1733, completed by van Nidek) is the ten-volume dictionary of the Republic of Letters; 61 wedding poems make him one of the most productive occasional poets of the age. The doorway is the poet whose thirty lines open Arnold's book. FLAG: Samuel's nephew, not his son (see the Network section); the RKD page gives his dates as "(1658-1724)" without days. Giorgio Vasari (30 July 1511 - 27 June 1574; dates search-verified only) - spare 5: the root of the whole genre: painter, architect (the Uffizi, begun 1560; the corridor, 1565) and the inventor of the encyclopedia of artistic biographies -- Le Vite (1550; partly rewritten, enlarged and given woodcut portraits, some imaginary, in 1568), dedicated to Cosimo I de' Medici, with its treatise on technique and its Florentine bias; van Mander and Sandrart both built their Italian sections on him, and Houbraken's book is the third generation of his invention. He is a spare, not a main, because his link to Arnold runs through van Mander and Sandrart -- two removes -- and his mechanism (canon-making by biography) is the series' background, not its next step. The doorway is the man who invented the book Arnold spent his life continuing. NOTE: the 13 mentions mean a later leg must not mistake familiarity for study -- he has never been a study subject. Cornelis de Bie (10 February 1627 - c. 1712-1715; dates search-verified only) - spare 6: the Antwerp rhetorician who wrote the South's answer to van Mander. Het Gulden Cabinet (Antwerp, 1662), commissioned by the publisher Jan Meyssens as "the South Netherlandish counterpart of Karel van Mander's Schilder-Boeck": three parts, 95 full-page engraved portraits, biographies and panegyrics of 16th- and 17th-century artists, dedicated to the collector Antoon van Leyen; "the principal source of information for later art historians such as Arnold Houbraken and Jacob Campo Weyerman". A notary and rederijker who deemed it his duty to broadcast the fame of the artists -- the panegyric as a system, against Houbraken's anecdotal biography. The doorway is the Antwerp rhetorician whose Golden Cabinet Houbraken mined. FLAG: death c. 1712-1715 (Wikipedia) vs 1715 (Commons) vs "after 1711" (Swann); publication 1661 (title-page plate) vs 1662 (colophon). Filippo Baldinucci (3 June 1625 - 10 January 1696; dates search-verified only) - spare 7: the Florentine who corrected Vasari with documents. Cardinal Leopoldo de' Medici's man, put in charge of the Medici drawing collection (the nucleus of the Uffizi's); Notizie de' professori del disegno (1681-1728, six volumes, the last three finished by his son Francesco Saverio), the first universal history of the figurative arts in Europe and "the first to make full use of documents"; the first history of engraving and the first dictionary of artistic terms (both 1686); dozens of Flemish and Dutch painters, including Rembrandt, judged worthy of inclusion. The doorway is the archivist who corrected Vasari with documents -- and wrote the first history of engraving. FLAG: death 1696, NOT 1697 (see the Network section); birth c. 1624 (Britannica, Columbia) vs 1625 (Uffizi, CalendarZ). Willem van Drielenburch (1632 Utrecht - c. 1687 Dordrecht; dates search-verified only) - spare 8: the rampjaar teacher. Bloemaert pupil, landscape painter in the manner of Jan Both; came to Dordrecht 1668; Arnold his pupil in 1672, the year of disaster, when the boy learned less painting than news -- his master "was constantly sending him out to hear the latest news". Also teacher of Wilhelmus Beurs. A small oeuvre and a thinner record than the other spares, but the anecdote is the mechanism: the pupil as messenger, the studio as a newsroom, the year the country nearly ended. The doorway is the teacher who sent his pupil out for the news in the year of disaster. (The 14 names carried in #545 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: George Vertue (1684 - 24 July 1756), Isaak Tirion (1705 in Utrecht - 1765 in Amsterdam), Jan van Gool (1685-1763) (mains); Jan Wagenaar (25 October 1709 - 1 March 1773) (reserve); Thomas Birch (1705-1766), Gerard Edelinck (bapt. 20 October 1640 - 2 April 1707) (alternates); spares: Cornelis Cort (c.1533-1578), Jonas Suyderhoef (1613-1686), Claes Jansz. Visscher (1587 - 19 June 1652), Nicolaes Visscher I (25 January 1618 - buried 11 September 1679), Samuel van Hoogstraten (1627-1678), Jacob Levecq (c.1634-1675), Aert Schouman (4 March 1710 - 5 July 1792), Bartholomeus van der Helst (1613 - buried 16 December 1670). George Vertue (1684 - 24 July 1756; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 123 lines) - the London engraver Houbraken superseded on the *Heads*. Apprenticed at 13, seven years under Michael Vandergucht, pupil of Thomas Gibson at Kneller's academy; Rose and Crown Club with Hogarth and Tillemans; official engraver to the Society of Antiquaries 1717, the only engraver made Fellow; ~500 portraits + ~500 antiquarian plates; *Twelve Heads of Poets* 1730; Rapin's *History of England* for the Knaptons 1736; his *Heads* contribution "submerged by the popularity of Houbraken's 'brilliant but less truthful productions'"; his forty volumes of notebooks, bought by Walpole, became *Anecdotes of Painting in England*. He is main 1 rather than top pick because he is the man Houbraken displaced, not the man Houbraken came from: the London half of the portrait trade, where Arnold is the Amsterdam root. The doorway is the London engraver whose plates Houbraken's brighter ones displaced. The parent confirms the promotion (see C561). Isaak Tirion (1705 in Utrecht - 1765 in Amsterdam; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 21 lines) - the publisher who turned Houbraken's faces into the national history. *Hedendaagsche historie* and *Vaderlandse Historie*, "illustrated with engravings by leading artists of Amsterdam"; moved to Amsterdam by May 1727; 1742 bought Wetstein's Kalverstraat house; *Het Verheerlykt Nederland* 1745-57, continued by his widow 1773-74. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the trade, not the hand: the man who commissioned the faces, where Arnold is the man who wrote the book that needed them. The doorway is the publisher who turned Houbraken's faces into the national history. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the sequel, not the source: the man who continued Arnold's book, where Arnold is the man who wrote it. The doorway is the sequel-writer whose new Schouburgh Houbraken illustrated. He is the reserve because he is the text, not the hand: the man who wrote the history Houbraken pictured, but whose own drawn work is nil. The doorway is the historian whose 21-part national history Houbraken illustrated for Tirion. Francois Morellon la Cave (15 April 1696 - 9 July 1768; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 39 lines) - the pupil who carried Picart's book-illustration trade into English history publishing. Huguenot engraver, settled The Hague; married Jeanne Bouffet 1722; engraving of Vivaldi 1725; illustrated Voltaire's Henriade and Tragedies, Garcilaso de la Vega's Comentarios Reales (Paris ed. 1731), Rapin de Thoyras's History of England vols I-X (1724-27); engravings after Hogarth and Coypel; portraits of Edward Pococke, Nader Shah, Prince William Duke of Cumberland, Willem de Fesch; active London c.1731-34 and c.1750; self-portrait 1765. He is main 1 rather than top pick because he is the pupil who left the workshop for London, not the collaborator who stayed in it: the trade carried outward, where Houbraken is the line carried forward. The doorway is the pupil who engraved Vivaldi and then illustrated the history of England. Gerard Hoet (22 August 1648 - 2 December 1733; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 185 lines) - the painter-engraver who designed the Bible Picart's shop engraved. 119 of the 214 picture-bible designs are his -- more than Picart's own 64; founded a drawing academy at Utrecht 1697 with Hendrick Schoock; published a book on drawing 1712 (103 prints by Pieter Bodart); source for Houbraken's Bentvueghels list; art dealer with Jacques Ignatius de Roore; trained by his father Moses Hoet and Warnard van Rijsen. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the designer behind the engraving, not the engraver: the man who drew the Bible and then taught a city to draw, where Houbraken is the man who engraved the faces. The doorway is the man who drew the Bible and then taught a city to draw. Etienne Picart le Romain (1632-1721; dates from search snippets only, not from a page read end to end) - the father, and the root of the line. Graveur du roi; Rome 1655-61 with Guillaume Vallet; pupil of Gilles Rousselet (not Francois de Poilly, per the British Museum); Academie Royale member 1664; last years from 1710 in the Netherlands, joining his son in Amsterdam; Bernard's first teacher. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the root before the trunk, not the continuation after it: the king's engraver whose son taught Amsterdam to engrave, where Houbraken is the son who carried the Amsterdam line on. The doorway is the king's engraver whose son taught Amsterdam to engrave. Prosper Marchand (11 March 1678 - 14 June 1756; dates from search snippets only, not from a page read end to end) - the bookseller-bibliographer who fled Paris with Picart and became the book-trade half of his world. Paris shop rue Saint-Jacques under the Phenix (with Gabriel II Martin); The Hague refuge with Picart Jan 1710; abandoned bookselling c.1713; proofreader for Fritsch & Bohm, Rotterdam; editor Journal litteraire 1713-1737; Dictionnaire historique (1758-59, posthumous); London 1726-1735; left his papers to Leiden University Library; with Picart and pupil Jakob van der Schley arranged illustrations for numerous books, supplying descriptions and rough sketches. He is the reserve because he is the trade, not the hand: the man who sketched the pictures Picart engraved, but whose own drawn work is the description, not the plate. The doorway is the refugee bookseller who sketched the pictures Picart engraved. (The 11 names carried in #543 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819), Hermanus Numan (1744-1820), Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783), Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826) (mains); Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809) (reserve); Hendrik Kobell (1751-1779), Jacques Kuyper (1761-1808) (alternates); spares: Pieter Barbiers II (1749-1842), Jacob Haafner (1754-1809), Jacob Otten Husly (16 November 1738 - 11 January 1796), Jan Bulthuis (1750-1801). Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses and taught the next engraver in the chain. Wall-covering and theatre-decoration painter; travelled to Brabant with Vinkeles and Izaak Schmidt in 1765; subject, with Schmidt and Vinkeles himself, of the 1765 triple portrait; teacher of Hermanus Numan at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the travelling companion, not the trunk: the painter beside the engraver on the road, whose wall-coverings are the large-scale cousin of Vinkeles's theatre sets. The doorway is the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses -- and taught the next engraver in the chain. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; the 20 "Andriessen" log mentions are all Louis Andriessen (1939-2021), the composer, studied in the Rzewski chain -- never Jurriaan; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Dates search-verified only (Alamy/WGA captions). Hermanus Numan (1744-1820; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. Engraver, copyist, theorist and publisher; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770; travelled to Paris in 1768, studied engraving there, then studied under Andriessen at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771; later Felix Meritis like Vinkeles and Vrijdag. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the theorist of the line, not its trunk: the man who wrote about engraving as well as engraving, and whose career runs parallel to Vinkeles's from the same Paris meeting. The doorway is the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia Hermanus Numan article, search-verified only (not read end to end in this run). FLAG: birth 1744 (EN Wikipedia lead) vs 1743 (KNAW, cited in the same article). Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn, and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. French engraver; head of the largest engraving workshop in Paris; teacher of Cochin, Cathelin, Strange and Ryland, as well as of Vinkeles for a year in 1770-71; Vinkeles lived in his house and learned his dry-needle technique there. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the French master behind the Dutch pupil: the technique itself, not the Amsterdam line -- and a study of him is a study of Paris, not of the chain that runs through Punt. The doorway is the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn -- and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia Jacques-Philippe Le Bas article, search-verified only (not read end to end in this run). Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. Engraver; "one of the few pupils Vinkeles accepted"; also trained in drawing by Steven Goble; gold medal at the Stadstekenacademie 1798; later professor/director of the Akademie van Beeldende Kunsten (1820); founder of the society Kunst Zij Ons Doel with Harmanus Fock and Harmanus Vinkeles; his 106-print Studiebeelden en Fragmenten (1807-16) is the next generation's drawing book; his 1812 etchings portray Jacobus Buys, Izaak Schmidt and Jurriaan Andriessen -- the Vinkeles circle, one generation on. He is main 5 rather than top pick because he is the continuation after Vinkeles, not the trunk before him: the pupil whose handling derives from Vinkeles's late manner, and the proof the school outlived the master. The doorway is the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the namesake met in Paris, a different de Wit from the master of the ceilings. Painter; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770. He is the reserve because he is a near-name collision with Jacob de Wit (study #541) and almost nothing is recorded beyond the Paris meeting -- but the meeting itself is dated and the confusion is worth one study to clear. The doorway is the namesake met in Paris -- a different de Wit from the master of the ceilings. Hendrik Kobell (1751-1779; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine etcher who died at twenty-eight, a fellow of the Pax circle. Marine painter and etcher; fellow member of Pax Artium Nutrix with Vinkeles; at the club he "became friendly with prominent collectors such as Jacob de Vos and Cornelis Ploos van Amstel". He is alternate 1 because his life is short and his oeuvre small, but his Pax membership and his marine etchings put him at the centre of the circle Vinkeles drank in. The doorway is the marine etcher who died at twenty-eight -- a fellow of the Pax circle. Draughtsman; with Pieter Barbiers II he drew the Felix Meritis drawing hall that Vinkeles and Vrijdag engraved in 1801; also a Barbiers collaborator. He is alternate 2 because he is the designer behind the engraving, not the engraver: the man whose drawing of a room became Vinkeles's largest sheet. Jacob Haafner (1754-1809; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the workshop apprentice who became the travel writer whose Ceylon book Vinkeles illustrated at sixty-five. Writer, not artist; apprenticed in Vinkeles's workshop (introduced by the merchant J. Hilkes), then travelled to India and Ceylon; Vinkeles made the original drawings for the only two illustrations to his Lotgevallen (1806); called the European discovery of India "the biggest disaster this country has been able to face". Jacob Otten Husly (16 November 1738 - 11 January 1796; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the architect who directed the drawing academy beside Vinkeles and built Felix Meritis. Architect; director of the Amsterdam Stadstekenacademie alongside Vinkeles; designed the Felix Meritis building on the Keizersgracht (with its drawing hall, engraved by Vinkeles in 1801), the Weesp City Hall and the Groningen City Hall; wrote a 1768 pamphlet on competitions for the city drawing academy. The doorway is the architect who directed the drawing academy beside Vinkeles -- and built Felix Meritis. (The 13 names carried in #542 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) (mains); Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) (reserve); Simon Fokke (1712-1784), Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) (alternates); spares: Jan Caspar Philips (1690-1775), Pieter Barbiers I (bapt. 23 April 1717 - 7 September 1780), Jan Baptist Xavery (Antwerp 1697 - 1742, The Hague), Gerard Joseph Xavery (27 July 1700 - after 1747), Jacob Folkema (1692-1767), Jan de Beijer (24 September 1703 - c.1780), Jacob van Huysum (bapt. 25 February 1688 - 1740), Caspar Jacobsz Philips (1732-1789). Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) - the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. Pupil of Norbert van Bloemen and of Jacobus Buys (carried #541 main); married Elisabeth Troost, daughter of Cornelis Troost (carried #541 main); a lumber merchant by trade and a collector of 5,000+ drawings including Rembrandts. He invented the prenttekening -- a transfer/offset method of reproducing drawings in the crayon manner, from 1765; his Hoofdwerk ran to 46 prints at 350 impressions each. His collection sold in 1800 for 109,406 guilders. His pupils included Christina Chalon, William V Prince of Orange, Elisabeth van Woensel and Hermanus Petrus Schouten; he reported Petrus Camper's facial-angle findings; a member of Arte et Amicitia. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the collector's answer to the engraver's question: not the man who made the prints, but the man who figured out how to print the drawings themselves, and the proof that the print trade had a second half Punt never touched. The doorway is the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. He appears in the #541 file only as network/provenance (leg-2 note, "ex Cornelis Ploos van Amstel collection"; patrons entry 27), never as a candidate. FLAG: one source gives "Print made by: Cornelis Ploos van Amstel in 1559-1760" -- an obvious typo for 1759-1760. Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) - the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. History painter who became an engraver; pupil of Gerard de Lairesse (carried #529 spare), Gerrit Rademaker (1672-1711) and Jacob van Huysum (proposed spare 7); sexton of the Amstelkerk from 1718; cooperated with Picart in 1729; made the title plate for van Gool's Nieuw Schouburg (1750); a central figure of the Stadstekenacademie circle (Folkema, Morellon, Troost, Fokke, Elgersma, Tanje); works at the Westerkerk, Nieuwe Kerk and Town Hall; "exchanged brush for graver"; his collection was auctioned in 1776; Ploos ranked him with Isaac de Moucheron, de Wit and Troost. He is the reserve because he is the hinge between the painter's trade and the engraver's: the man who made the same crossing Punt made in reverse (Punt went from needle to brush late; Dubourg went from brush to graver), but his own engraved oeuvre is thin and his dates rest on one article. The doorway is the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Simon Fokke (1712-1784) - the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. Pupil of J. C. Philips (proposed spare 1); the booksellers' engraver of small portraits and vignettes; plates for Wagenaar's Vaderlandsche Historie 1749-59; Danish portraits for Tycho Hofman (1741); after Vernet, Avercamp, Terborch, Ribera and Troost (a burlesque Death of Dido); the title vignette for Rousseau's Discourse on Inequality (1755); his portrait was painted by John Greenwood after Jacobus Buys. The doorway is the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) - the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Pupil of Picart from about age 12; completed unfinished plates after Picart's death; made the memorial portrait of Picart (1734); maps for Prevost's Histoire generale des voyages (publisher Pieter de Hondt, The Hague); illustrations for La Vie de Marianne (1735-47); Brantome frontispieces (1740); L'Imprimerie (1739); taught Philippus van der Schley (1724-1817); cooperated with Prosper Marchand. The doorway is the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Jacobus Buys (19 November 1724 - 7 April 1801; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the wig-maker's son who studied under all three of Amsterdam's masters and ended up running its drawing academy. Painter and engraver; studied under Cornelis Pronk, Jacob de Wit and Cornelis Troost; director of the Amsterdam Drawing Academy; joined the Guild of St Luke in 1750; bas-reliefs, tapestry designs, book illustrations and theatre scenes. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the pupil line after the engraver: the man who learned the witje, the flower piece and the theatre scene from three different masters and turned them into a teaching post. The doorway is the wig-maker's son who studied under all three of Amsterdam's masters and ended up running its drawing academy. Tako Hajo Jelgersma (29 October 1702 - 24 March 1795; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the follower who painted de Wit's grey stone for another forty years, and outlived him by forty-one. Painter; de Wit's follower in grisaille; pupil of Frans Decker and Wigerus Vitringa; moved to Haarlem in 1752; pupils Johan Bernard Brandhoff, Johannes Petrus van Horstok, Cornelis van Noorde, Martinus van der Jagt and Johannes Swertner; lived to 92. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the influence line itself, narrow and documented: the only named man who kept painting grisailles in de Wit's manner after de Wit was gone, and the proof that the witje outlived its inventor. The doorway is the follower who painted de Wit's grey stone for another forty years -- and outlived him by forty-one. (The 14 names carried in #540 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem), Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. FLAG: the page says earliest dated work 1660 and last dated work 1695, against burial 24 January 1694 - the 1695 date cannot stand as painted in his lifetime; also the 1659 "pupil of an Everdingen" record is assumed to be Caesar van Everdingen, not proven. Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left. German-born Dutch painter, draughtsman, calligrapher and printmaker, the leading Dutch marine painter after both Willem van de Veldes left for England in 1672. He came to Amsterdam around 1650 as a merchant's clerk, taught himself to draw ships, studied under Allaert van Everdingen and Hendrick Dubbels, joined the painters' guild in 1663, and became famous for violent water - he went out to sea in an open boat to study storms. He painted the great View of Amsterdam and the IJ (1665) as a present for Louis XIV's minister, opened a gallery on the top floor of the Amsterdam town hall in 1699, and was visited by Cosimo III de' Medici and Peter the Great. His pupils and followers include Jan Claesz Rietschoof, Aernout Smit, Michiel Maddersteg and Abraham Storck. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the hub of the whole marine spare set - three of this study's spares are his pupils or followers - so studying him unlocks all of them, and his open-boat storm study is a mechanism no other candidate offers. The doorway is the clerk-turned-calligrapher who rowed out into storms in an open boat to learn how water breaks, and then taught half of Amsterdam's marine painters. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Dutch Golden Age seascape painter; pupil of Abraham Liedts, then of Ludolf Bakhuizen; active in Hoorn all his career, possibly some time in Amsterdam with Bakhuizen; taught his son Hendrik; one of Bakhuizen's most accomplished pupils (Sotheby's, on a stormy sea after Bakhuizen's Fishing Vessels Offshore in a Heavy Sea, 1684, completed 1685); works in the Rijksmuseum, Het Scheepvaartmuseum, Westfries Museum Hoorn, the Hermitage, the National Maritime Museum Greenwich; Hoorn contemporary of the flower painter Jacob Rootius, son of Jan Albertsz Rotius. Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. The doorway is the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions as a study subject; no local file; not carried in #522-#539 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: teacher conflict - pupil of Peter Verschatfelt (New International Encyclopaedia) vs early training under Egid Verhelst (Taylor Graham, Wikipedia). Both may be true at different stages; no source read reconciles them. Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. German painter, etcher and draftsman; brother of Ferdinand, uncle and teacher of Wilhelm; sent to Italy by Elector Karl Theodor 1776, in Rome until 1785, working from nature and monumental buildings; court painter at Munich; produced only a few oil paintings, highly praised by Goethe (Rocky Landscape with Waterfalls, once in the Bamberg gallery), but left 20,000 landscape and architecture pen drawings and etchings, painting being too slow to keep pace with his ideas. The doorway is the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions as a study subject; no local file; not carried in #522-#539 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: Johann Christian Ludwig Heydenreich is named as his teacher only by WahooArt; unverified elsewhere. Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. German landscape painter, pioneer of Naturalism; trained under Johann Jakob Dorner the elder and at the Munich Zeichnungsakademie; inspector of the Hofgarten Galerie 1790; professor of landscape painting at the Munich Academy 1808-1814, replaced by Wilhelm von Kobell in 1813/1814; central gallery director 1822, founding director of what became the Alte Pinakothek; mentor of Carl Rottmann and Ludwig Richter; insisted on Naturstudium, direct study of nature, against the history-painting hierarchy. The doorway is the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Baptist Boel the Younger (between September and 4 October 1643, Antwerp - 9 January 1689; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S22). Son and pupil of Pieter Boel; family moved to Paris 1668 for the Gobelins; married Maria Catharina Immenraet, daughter of landscape painter Philips Augustijn Immenraet, 4 May 1675; collaborated with other Antwerp painters, adding the still-life and animal elements to their compositions; known oeuvre very limited; assimilated his father's style so completely that attribution problems follow (Nationalmuseum). Held back because his oeuvre is small and mostly indistinguishable from his father's. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff (also Blankerhoff/Blankhof/Jan Maat; 10 January 1628, Alkmaar - bur. 2 October 1669, Amsterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S26). Marine painter; taught by Arent Teerling, Pieter Scheyenburg and Caesar van Everdingen; served on a Dutch warship; Rome, Bentvueghels, called Jan Maat; Italian ports with strange boats his most popular pieces; teacher of Aernout Smit; Battle of the Zuiderzee 1663-1668, 700 guilders, Rijksmuseum SK-A-3235. Held back because his career is short (work period 1649-1669) and his record is thin. Hendrik Kobell (13 September 1751, Rotterdam - 3 August 1779, Rotterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S29). Dutch landscape and marine painter, etcher, draftsman, watercolourist; cousin of Ferdinand and Franz Kobell; father trained him in drawing, he loved drawing ships in Rotterdam port; London 1769-1770, Paris 1772, Amsterdam Stadstekenakademie; founder of the Rotterdam drawing society Hierdoor tot Hooger; father and teacher of Jan Kobell II (1778-1814); pupils Carel Frederik Bendorp and Gerrit van der Pals; The Shipwreck (1775, Rijksmuseum SK-A-1218). New names from Kobell's own network -- verified NOT STUDIED, for a later round (NOT part of the 8 extra spares above): Carl Rottmann (11 January 1797, Handschuhsheim - 7 July 1850, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source). German landscape painter, most famous of the Rottmann family. First drawing lessons from his father Friedrich, who taught drawing at Heidelberg University; moved to Munich in 1821/1822, married Friederike von Sckell in 1824, and through her came to the attention of Crown Prince Ludwig, who sent him to Italy in 1826/1827 to sketch the 28 Italian landscapes in fresco for the Hofgarten arcades (completed 1833), then commissioned a Greek cycle in 1834 (23 large landscapes, now in the Neue Pinakothek). Court painter in 1841. His school included Karl Lindemann-Frommel, Karl Ludwig Seeger, August Loffler and August Seidel. WHY: the Munich landscapist who raised landscape to the height of history painting in fresco - the direct successor to Dillis's and Kobell's Munich. The doorway is: from Dillis's Munich, hop to the painter Ludwig sent to Italy to fresco a whole arcade of landscapes. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file (parent verified both). Peter von Cornelius (23 September 1783, Dusseldorf - 6 March 1867, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source). German painter, a main representative of the Nazarene movement. Drawing classes at the Dusseldorf Academy from age twelve; Rome 1811-1819 with the Lukasbund; summoned to Munich by Crown Prince Ludwig in 1819 to decorate the Glyptothek; director of the Dusseldorf Academy in 1821 and of the Munich Academy from 1824/1825; his "Last Judgment" (1829-1840) fills the east wall of the Ludwigskirche in Munich, 19 m by 12 m. His pupils included Karl Hermann, Wilhelm von Kaulbach and Adam Eberle; he left Munich for Berlin in 1839-1840. WHY: the academy director who rebuilt German painting around fresco and the Munich school - the institutional successor to Kobell's generation. The doorway is: from Kobell's Munich Academy, hop to the Nazarene who took its directorship and covered a church wall with the Last Judgment. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file (the file cornelius-cardew.md in the studies directory is Cornelius Cardew, a different person; parent verified both).



(The 14 names carried in #539 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam), Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - 1694, Haarlem), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam. Dutch Rococo painter and interior decorator; pupil of Albert van Spiers and Jacob van Hal, Antwerp Guild of St Luke 1714; copied Rubens's ceilings in the Carolus Borromeuskerk in watercolour 1711-1722, the record after lightning destroyed them in 1718 (engraved by his pupil Jan Punt, 1751); famous for door and ceiling paintings in Amsterdam canal houses (he lived on the Keizersgracht) and country houses on the Vecht; grisailles he called "witjes" - grey-white paintings faking stone relief; Moses Choosing the Seventy Elders (1737, Amsterdam Town Hall), Apotheosis of Aeneas for Pieter Pels (Herengracht 479), ceiling with Isaac de Moucheron; pupils Jan Punt, Pieter Tanje, Dionys van Nijmegen, Jan de Groot, Frans and Jacob Xavery; follower Tako Hajo Jelgersma. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the Moucheron line's other half - the figure painter inside the wall-painter's room - and the witje is the one trompe-l'oeil mechanism this chain has never studied. The doorway is the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: one conservation page heads him "(1685-1754)" while its own body gives baptized 19 December 1695 - the 1685 is a typo. Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: the page says earliest dated work 1660 and last dated work 1695, against burial 24 January 1694 - the 1695 date cannot stand as painted in his lifetime; also the 1659 "pupil of an Everdingen" record is assumed to be Caesar van Everdingen, not proven. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. Marine painter; pupil of Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff; painted in an attic room on the Singel, probably employed by dealer Laurens Cornelisz de Coninck; follower and copyist of Ludolf Bakhuizen (1687/1689 notarial records of copying Bakhuizen); The Africa in Table Bay; most marines 1667-1678, later style tending to Bakhuizen; Amsterdam Guild of St Luke 1688; died aged about 70, buried at the Leiden cemetery. Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who built his patron real ships. Marine painter; studied under Ludolf Bakhuizen; spent most of his life at the court of Frederick I of Prussia, returned to Amsterdam later in life, where he commissioned the building of two beautiful ships for Frederick I's pleasure; most of his paintings are in Germany. The doorway is the marine painter who built his patron real ships. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Dutch Golden Age seascape painter; pupil of Abraham Liedts, then of Ludolf Bakhuizen; active in Hoorn all his career, possibly some time in Amsterdam with Bakhuizen; taught his son Hendrik; one of Bakhuizen's most accomplished pupils (Sotheby's, on a stormy sea after Bakhuizen's Fishing Vessels Offshore in a Heavy Sea, 1684, completed 1685); works in the Rijksmuseum, Het Scheepvaartmuseum, Westfries Museum Hoorn, the Hermitage, the National Maritime Museum Greenwich; Hoorn contemporary of the flower painter Jacob Rootius, son of Jan Albertsz Rotius. The doorway is the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - 1694, Haarlem; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Baptist Boel the Younger (between September and 4 October 1643, Antwerp - 9 January 1689; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S22). Son and pupil of Pieter Boel; family moved to Paris 1668 for the Gobelins; married Maria Catharina Immenraet, daughter of landscape painter Philips Augustijn Immenraet, 4 May 1675; collaborated with other Antwerp painters, adding the still-life and animal elements to their compositions; known oeuvre very limited; assimilated his father's style so completely that attribution problems follow (Nationalmuseum). Held back because his oeuvre is small and mostly indistinguishable from his father's. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff (also Blankerhoff/Blankhof/Jan Maat; 10 January 1628, Alkmaar - bur. 2 October 1669, Amsterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S26). Marine painter; taught by Arent Teerling, Pieter Scheyenburg and Caesar van Everdingen; served on a Dutch warship; Rome, Bentvueghels, called Jan Maat; Italian ports with strange boats his most popular pieces; teacher of Aernout Smit; Battle of the Zuiderzee 1663-1668, 700 guilders, Rijksmuseum SK-A-3235. Held back because his career is short (work period 1649-1669) and his record is thin. Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 - 7 November 1708; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left: clerk and calligrapher turned painter, student of Everdingen then Dubbels, who went out in an open boat to study storms and became the leading Dutch marine painter after 1672. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the pupil first, and Bakhuizen is the sea line, not the cattle line. The doorway is the clerk who went out in an open boat to study storms. He is main 3 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the pupil and the marine line, not through the menagerie. The doorway is the man who painted from live animals at Versailles. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is Boel's fellow pupil of the menagerie line, and the study should meet Boel first. The doorway is the Snyders pupil who drew 52 species for the king. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the mirror water, the collector as painter) is the most specialised in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. (The 14 names carried in #537 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jan Weenix (1642-1719), Frans Snyders (11 November 1579 - 19 August 1657), Paul de Vos (1591/92 or 1595 - 30 June 1678) (mains); Melchior d'Hondecoeter (c. 1636 - 3 April 1695) (reserve); Jan Hackaert (bapt. February 1628 - in/after 1685), Willem van de Velde the Younger (bapt. 18 December 1633 - 6 April 1707) (alternates); spares: Gerrit Berckheyde (bapt. 6 June 1638 - 10 June 1698), Pieter de Molijn (bapt. 6 April 1595 - 23 March 1661), Caesar van Everdingen (1616/17 - bur. 13 October 1678), Pieter de Grebber (c. 1600 - 1652/53), Willem Cornelisz Duyster (1599-1635), Salomon van Ruysdael (c. 1602 - bur. 3 November 1670), Willem van de Velde the Elder (c. 1611 - 13 December 1693), Jan Fyt (bapt. 15 March 1611 - 11 September 1661). Jan Weenix (1642-1719; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the hunt line carried into dead-game still life. Trained by his father Jan Baptist Weenix (carried #531) with his cousin Melchior d'Hondecoeter; dead game and hunting scenes; Utrecht guild 1664 and 1668; painted Peter the Great 1697; court painter to Elector Palatine Johann Wilhelm 1702-1712/14, 12 large hunting pictures for Schloss Bensberg; pupils his daughter Maria Weenix and Dirk Valkenburg (carried #536 extra spare); Goethe devoted a poem to his animal textures; the Wallace Collection holds 13 paintings. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the copy line runs through the cattle painter first, and Weenix is the still-life end of the hunt, not its living middle. The doorway is the hunter who became the still-life painter of dead game. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: birth year uncertain - sources give between 1641 and 1649; 1642 is the commonly cited year; the page notes he married in 1679 "around thirty" and had 13 children 1680-1700. Frans Snyders (11 November 1579 - 19 August 1657; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Antwerp root of the whole hunt and animal line. Pupil of Pieter Brueghel the Younger 1593, then Hendrick van Balen; master 1602; Italy 1608-09 (Rome, Milan, Cardinal Borromeo); married Margaretha de Vos 1611 (sister of Cornelis and Paul de Vos); Rubens collaboration from the 1610s; Torre de la Parada 1636-38, about 60 hunting and animal pieces after Rubens designs, plus 18 more 1639; dean 1628; pupils Nicasius Bernaerts, Peter van Boucle, Juriaen Jacobsze, Jan Roos, Paul de Vos; Jan Fyt student and assistant from 1629; patrons Archduke Albert VII, Philip IV of Spain, Bishop Antonius Triest; influenced Jan Davidsz. de Heem and the 18th-century French animaliers Oudry and Desportes; the concert of birds theme; died childless. He is main 3 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the son's copying, not through Snyders directly, and his scale would swamp the Haarlem chain. The doorway is the Antwerp root of the hunt. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Paul de Vos (1591/92 or 1595 - 30 June 1678; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Snyders's brother-in-law and workshop colleague; the second Antwerp hunt painter. Pupil of Denijs van Hove 1604, David Remeeus 1605, Eduard Snayers; worked in his brother-in-law Frans Snyders' workshop (Snyders married his sister Margaretha 1611); guild master 1620; monumental hunts for aristocrats, warmer colours and broader brush than Snyders, fighting cats, horses attacked by wolves; collaborator with Rubens (Torre de la Parada), Van Dyck, Jan Wildens; patrons Marquis of Leganes and Philippe-Charles 3rd Count of Arenberg (36+ paintings 1633-1640); many works long misattributed to Snyders; never dated his paintings. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is Snyders's shadow, and the study should meet Snyders first. The doorway is the brother-in-law in Snyders's shadow who painted the fighting cats. Reserve: Melchior d'Hondecoeter (c. 1636 - 3 April 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the bird painter: grandson of Gillis, son of Gijsbert, nephew of J. B. Weenix, cousin of Jan Weenix; trained by his father Gijsbert then his uncle J. B. Weenix; Confrerie Pictura 1659; married Susanne Tradel 1663; painted almost exclusively birds (exotic and game) in park landscapes; about 250 works, only about 20 dated; pupils Willem Hendrik Wilhelmus van Royen and Jan Weenix; employed by William III to paint his menagerie at Het Loo; his inventory lists a small gallows (to pose birds) and seven Snyders paintings; followers include Adriaen Coorte, Jan van Huysum, Elias Vonck; early sea-pieces and Tub with Fish 1655 before switching to birds. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the bird as still life in a park landscape, the menagerie as subject) is the most specialised in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the bird painter with a gallows in his inventory. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. February 1628 - in/after 1685; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - forest landscapes with hunting scenes, more original than his Italianates. Germany and Switzerland, Lake of Zurich c. 1660-64 (long mistaken for an Italian lake); influenced by Jan Both (studied #533) and Asselijn (carried #532); staffage by Berchem, Adriaen van de Velde, Lingelbach; painted landscape backgrounds for Berchem and van de Velde; owned and copied Jan Both works; latest signed work 1685. The doorway is the forest painter whose Swiss lake was mistaken for Italy. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Willem van de Velde the Younger (bapt. 18 December 1633 - 6 April 1707; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine line: son of Willem the Elder, elder brother of Adriaen; trained by his father then Simon de Vlieger (Weesp); Amsterdam until 1672, then England with his father, Charles II service, studio at Queen's House Greenwich, Westminster 1691; leading Dutch marine painter of the later 17th century. He is an alternate rather than a main because the marine line is a step sideways from the hunt, and his brother is the top pick. The doorway is the elder brother who painted the sea while the younger painted the cattle. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 8 below): Gerrit Berckheyde (bapt. 6 June 1638 - 10 June 1698; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Haarlem; younger brother and pupil of Job Berckheyde; Rhine trip 1650s, Heidelberg court of Charles I Louis, golden medal; Haarlem Guild 27 July 1660; RKD: Italianate landscapes, portraits, cavalry pieces; influenced by Pieter Saenredam; staffage possibly by Lingelbach and Jan van Huchtenburg; no pupils documented; influenced Jan ten Compe and Isaac Ouwater; views of the Grote Markt and Grote Kerk Haarlem, Amsterdam Town Hall, The Hague. His is the study of the Haarlem townscape painter who also painted cavalry pieces, the town answer to Oudendijck's camp. The doorway is the Haarlem townscape painter the son followed. Pieter de Molijn (bapt. 6 April 1595 London - 23 March 1661 Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Guild Haarlem 1616, dean 1633, 1638, 1646; tonal-phase pioneer with Jan van Goyen and Salomon van Ruysdael; pupils Gerard ter Borch the Younger and Elder, Allaert van Everdingen, Jan Wils, Jan Coelenbier; collaborated with ter Borch, Frans Hals, Pieter de Grebber, Jacob de Wet, Jacob Pynas, Nicolaes Berchem; son Anthony de Molijn (1635-c. 1703). His is the study of the tonal pioneer who taught the generation before Oudendijck. The doorway is the tonal pioneer who taught ter Borch and Everdingen. His is the study of the tonal river painter who taught his nephew. The doorway is the uncle who taught Ruisdael the river. Willem van de Velde the Elder (c. 1611 - 13 December 1693; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - pen paintings (penschilderij) in black ink on vellum, panel and canvas; sailed with the fleet sketching battles (Scheveningen 1653, Sole Bay 1672, about 70 sketches); Charles II paid him 100 pounds a year for "draughts of sea fights" and the same to his son to colour them; father of Willem the Younger and Adriaen, and daughter Magdalena (b. 1632); Amsterdam studio on Nieuwe Waalseiland; commissions from Admiral Cornelis Tromp; died London 1693, buried St James's Piccadilly. His is the study of the father who drew sea battles in pen while his sons painted them in oil. The doorway is the father who sketched the fleet in pen. Jan Fyt (bapt. 15 March 1611 - 11 September 1661; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Snyders's pupil and assistant 1629-31, master 1629/30; Italy 1633-41 (Paris, Venice for the Sagredo and Contarini families, Rome 1635, Bentvueghels bent-name "Goudvink", bullfinch); back in Antwerp by 1641; Guild of Romanists dean 1652; married Francoise van de Sande 22 March 1654, four children; about 380 paintings (287 extant still lifes per Christie's); moved the dead-game display from the indoor table to the open landscape, the first to paint game as a trophy of the hunt rather than food; pupils Pieter Boel and Jacob van der Kerckhoven; collaborators Cornelius Schut, Thomas Willeboirts Bosschaert, Erasmus Quellinus II; Set of the Dogs 1642 dedicated to Don Carlo Guasco, Marquess of Soleno. His is the study of the man who took dead game out of the kitchen and into the landscape. The doorway is the pupil who moved the game from the table to the field. Philips Koninck (5 November 1619 - 4 October 1688; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Amsterdam; younger brother and pupil of Jacob Koninck; Houbraken says he completed his training in Rembrandt's studio (Britannica doubts it); from the late 1640s the panoramic landscapes, canvas split half earth and half sky, bands of shade and light after Rembrandt's etchings and Hercules Segers; figures by Adriaen van de Velde in Woodland border and countryside; about 300 drawings; owned a barge line Amsterdam-Rotterdam via Leiden; stopped painting after 1676. Jan van der Heyden (5 March 1637 - 28 March 1712; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Gorinchem-Amsterdam; one of the first Dutch painters to specialise in townscapes and a leading architectural painter of the Golden Age; his most frequent collaborator was Adriaen van de Velde; designed and implemented Amsterdam's street-lighting scheme (1669-1840, adopted abroad); married Sara ter Hiel 1661; 14 paintings connected with Maarssen, probably for Joan Huydecoper II; died wealthy, over 70 of his own paintings in his estate; Room Corner with Curiosities painted at 75 in 1712, the year he died. Simon de Vlieger (c. 1601 Rotterdam - bur. 13 March 1653 Weesp; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - marine painter; Delft Guild 1634; early work after Jan Porcellis (his teacher per the Royal Museums Greenwich), then his own silvery light and cool palette; teacher of Willem van de Velde the Younger (c. 1648-1650) and, per one low-quality source, of Adriaen van de Velde; the bridge between the second and third generations of Dutch marine painters; also influenced Hendrick Dubbels and Jan van de Cappelle. Jan Porcellis (1580/84 Ghent - 29 January 1632 Zoeterwoude; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - marine painter; initiated the decisive transition from early realism to the tonal phase: overcast skies, rough waters, low horizons, two-thirds of the canvas sea and sky; supposed pupil of Hendrick Vroom (per Houbraken); Antwerp guild 1617, Rotterdam by 1615, Haarlem around 1622; teacher of Simon de Vlieger per the Royal Museums Greenwich; the most influential marine painter of the first half of the 17th century; only about ten works securely identified. Hercules Segers (c. 1589 - c. 1638; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Haarlem-born; pupil of Gillis van Coninxloo; the most inspired, experimental and original landscapist of his period and an even more innovative printmaker; 54 plates, 183 known impressions, about 15 paintings; printed in coloured ink on coloured paper and cloth, hand-coloured, cropped to different sizes, each impression a miniature painting; invented lift-ground (sugar-bite) etching, not used again for 150 years; Rembrandt owned eight of his paintings and a printing plate; art dealer by trade; wife recorded as a widow in 1638. (The 14 names carried in #536 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Salomon de Bray (1597 - 11 May 1664), Leendert van der Cooghen (9 May 1632 - 18 February 1681), Dirk Maas (12 September 1659 - 25 December 1717) (mains); Jan van Huchtenburg (bapt. 20 November 1647 - bur. 2 July 1733) (reserve); Jan Wyck (29 October 1645 - 17 May 1702), Jacob Gole (1660-1737) (alternates); spares: Egbert van Heemskerck the Elder (1634 - c. 1702/04), Evert Collier (26 January 1642 - few days before 8 September 1708), Adriaen Oudendijck (1677-1704), Jacob Jordaens (19 May 1593 - 18 October 1678), Rachel Ruysch (3 June 1664 - 12 October 1750), Jan van Gool (1685-1763), Gerrit Claesz Bleker (1592 - 8 February 1656), John Wootton (c. 1682 - 13 November 1764). Salomon de Bray (1597 - 11 May 1664; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the draftsman whose figure studies Dusart kept: they sit in Dusart's 1704 probate inventory alongside Bega's 125 figure studies, so Dusart the collector valued de Bray's drawing as highly as his own master's circle. He is also the Haarlem classicist against whom the whole peasant line defines itself: history painter, architect of the City Hall and the Zijlpoort, town planner, poet, designer of silverwork, author of Architectura Moderna (1631), headman of the Guild of St. Luke who tried and failed to rewrite its charter in 1631 to put painting above the crafts. He died of plague in 1664 with four of his children; his sons Jan, Dirck and Joseph carried the name on, Jan already carried as a #535 spare. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he predates Dusart by a generation and the hop runs through the probate inventory, not through a shared master. The doorway is the classicist whose figure studies Dusart kept in his own inventory. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Leendert van der Cooghen (9 May 1632 - 18 February 1681; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the other draftsman in Dusart's probate inventory, and the closest parallel to Dusart himself as a draughtsman: an amateur of independent means who never had to sell, about 66 drawings and only 3 certain paintings, figure and portrait studies in red and black chalk drawn directly from the life model, dated day-month-year on 46 sheets so a chronology can be built, moving from a static dry style to a looser freer one. He trained with Jacob Jordaens, shared a studio with Cornelis Bega, befriended Vincent van der Vinne, and never married - "uncle Leendert" of the Schagelstraat, the bachelor counterpart to Dusart the bachelor who lived with his aunts. His mechanism set (the dated single-figure chalk study, the amateur who draws only when he feels like it) is the direct mirror of Dusart's own single-peasant chalks. The doorway is the amateur whose dated chalk studies Dusart collected and whose life mirrors his own. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Dirk Maas (12 September 1659 - 25 December 1717; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil-chain hop with a direct Dusart document behind it: he was back in Haarlem in May 1692 on the testimony of Cornelis Dusart himself (Bredius), and he is the painter who took the Haarlem line to war - pupil of Mommers, then Berchem, then Jan van Huchtenburg, then painter of the Battle of the Boyne first-hand in 1690 for William III and for his patron Hans Willem Bentinck, Earl of Portland. He also did staffage for other painters and worked in the same studio as Adriaen Oudendijck 1695-1700, so he ties the Oudendijck top pick to the Huchtenburg reserve and the Wyck alternate in one knot. The doorway is the horse painter Dusart himself testified for, who painted the Boyne first-hand. Reserve: Jan van Huchtenburg (bapt. 20 November 1647 - bur. 2 July 1733; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the battle machine of the network: first taught by Thomas Wijck, then trained in Paris under Adam Frans van der Meulen at the Gobelins, then court painter to Prince Eugene of Savoy with a ten-canvas series of Eugene's battles from Zenta (1697) to Belgrade (1717), plus William III's campaigns. He taught Dirk Maas, collaborated with Jan Wyck, assisted Gerrit Berckheyde by painting his people and horses, and his pupils Alexander van Gaelen and Izaak Vogelensanck carried the Wouwerman battle style into England. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the monumental multi-figure battle composition, the court-painter campaign series) is the largest scale in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the court painter who turned ten of Prince Eugene's battles into one series. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Wyck (29 October 1645 - 17 May 1702; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the son of Thomas Wijck who carried the Haarlem line to England and founded British military and sporting painting: over 150 works, the Battle of the Boyne (1690) painted half a dozen times, William III's campaigns through the Nine Years' War, and as a teacher the master of John Wootton and Martin Beckman. He accompanied Dirk Maas to Ireland to paint William's campaigns, and his battle pieces bring the viewer into the fight at eye level instead of the prevailing bird's-eye disposition of troops. He is an alternate rather than a main because the date conflict needs settling before he can be a top pick. The doorway is the Haarlem battle painter who founded British sporting art from Mortlake.
